Insertion algorithms for Type B \mathrm{B} and D \mathrm{D} Gelfand W W -graphs
Yifeng ZHANG

Abstract
Like the RSK correspondence for symmetric groups, Garfinkle defined a domino correspondence for type B \mathrm{B} and D \mathrm{D} Coxeter groups. Similar to the Knuth relations, Taskin and Pietraho give the plactic relations for the domino correspondence and Bonnafé use them to classify the cells for type B \mathrm{B} Coxeter groups. We give some further properties of the plactic relations and use these relations to describe the bidirected edges and the molecules of Gelfand W W -graphs for type B \mathrm{B} and D \mathrm{D} Coxeter groups.



1
Introduction

The simplest form of the
RSK correspondence

is a bijection
w\mapsto(P_{\mathsf{RSK}}(w),Q_{\mathsf{RSK}}(w))
from permutations to pairs of standard Young tableaux of same shape. This correpondence divided the permutations into equivalence classes indexed by various standard Young diagrams. It plays a very important roles in combinatorics and representation theory. In 1979, Kazhdan and Lusztig
[
13
]
give a different description of this equivalence classes by defining so-called Kazhdan-Lusztig bases of Iwahori-Hecke algebra
\mathcal{H}
and its corresponding
W
-graphs. Then we call this equivalence classes (left) cells. In the
W
-graph, we define a cell to be its strong-connected components.

The notion of Kazhdan-Lusztig basis and
W
-graphs are generalized by us to Iwahori-Hecke algebra modules
\mathcal{M}
and
\mathcal{N}

[
17
]
. We also defined two corresponding
W
-graphs
[
19
]
. A
molecule
of the
W
-graph is a connected component of the graph with only the bidirected edges. In Type A case, we classified the molecules to see that they are just the equivalence classes above. Moreover, we conjectured that the cells are just the molecules.

In type B case, the weight function of group
W^{\mathsf{BC}}_{n}
is determined by a parameter
s
. When
s=1
, the cell are classified by Garfinkle
[
9
]
. When
s>n-1
, the case are solved by Bonnafé and Iancu
[
3
,
6
]
. For the general case, Bonnafé, Geck, Iancu, and Lam
[
5
]
conjectured that the cells coincide with the combinatorial cells defined by the
domino correspondence
. The
domino correspondence
is given by Garfinkle
[
9
]
, and it gives a bijection between
W^{\mathsf{BC}}_{n}
and the set of a pair of same shape standard domino tableau. Similar to the Knuth relation for RSK correspondence, the domino correspondence has its own plactic/coplactic relations, given by Taskin
[
25
]
and Pietraho
[
21
]
. Using these relations, Bonnafé
[
4
]
proved half of the conjecture: the cells are unions of the combinatorial cells. In type D case, Gern
[
11
]
use the domino corrrspondence to study the cells of
W^{\mathsf{D}}_{n}
.

Except for the domino corrrspondence, there is another bijective correpondence mapping between
W^{\mathsf{BC}}_{n}
and the set of a pair of same shape standard bitableau, given by Bonnafé
[
3
]
. He also gives the relative plactic/coplactic relation for this correspondence.

In this paper, we further discuss these two correspondences and use them to describe the bidirected edges in Gelfand
W^{\mathsf{BC}}_{n}
-graphs. We first review the definition of signed permutations in Section
2
. In Section
3
, we review the domino insertions and the plactic relation given by Taskin. Moreover, we give the relation of recording domino tableaux while the corresponding signed permutation are related by a plactic relation. In Section
4
, we review Bonnafé’s bitableau correspondence and define a modified version of it. Finally, we move to Gelfand
W^{\mathsf{BC}}_{n}
-graphs and Gelfand
W^{\mathsf{D}}_{n}
-graphs. We describe the bidirected edges and the molecules in Section
5
.

2
Preliminary

We know that the Coxeter system of type
B_{n}
can be expressed as
(W^{\mathsf{BC}}_{n},S)
where
S=\{s_{0},s_{1},\cdots,s_{n-1}\}
. Here
s_{0}=(-1,1)
and
s_{i}=(-i-1,-i)(i,i+1)
for
i>0
. Then for
w\in W^{\mathsf{BC}}_{n}
, we can define the
reduced expression

w=s_{1}s_{2}\cdots s_{l}
with
s_{i}\in S
. We call
l
the
length
of
w
and denote it as
\ell(w)
.

w\in W^{\mathsf{BC}}_{n}
can also be interpreted as a signed permutation.
A
signed permutation

w\in W^{\mathsf{BC}}_{n}
is a permutation of
-[n]\cup[n]
such that
w(-i)=-w(i)
, where
[n]=\{1,2,\cdots,n\}
.
w\in W^{\mathsf{BC}}_{n}
can be denoted as
one-line notation
,
w=w_{1}w_{2}\cdots w_{n}
where
w_{i}=w(i)

for
1\leq i\leq n
and
\{|w_{1}|,|w_{2}|,\cdots,|w_{n}|\}=\{1,2,\cdots,n\}
. In the following we set the following representation for all integers:
\bar{a}=-a
.

We denote by
\leq
the
Bruhat order
on
W^{\mathsf{BC}}_{n}
defined by the set of generators
S
. We write
x<y
to say that
x\leq y
and
x\neq y
.

Using the Bruhat order, we define the
left descents
of
w
as follows
\mathrm{Des}_{L}(w):=\{i\in[0,n-1]|\ell(s_{i}w)<\ell(w)\}
.

A
signed involution
is an element
z\in W^{\mathsf{BC}}_{n}
such that
z^{2}=1\in W^{\mathsf{BC}}_{n}
.

Type
D_{n}
Coxeter group
W^{\mathsf{D}}_{n}
is a subgroup of
W^{\mathsf{BC}}_{n}
such that
w\in W^{\mathsf{D}}_{n}
has even number of
i\in[n]
with
w(i)<0
. It is also a Coxeter group, with simple transiposition
\{s_{-1},s_{1},\cdots,s_{n-1}
where
s_{-1}=(-2,1)(-1,2)
.

3
Domino correspondence

3.1
Domino tableaux

Let
\lambda=(\lambda_{1},\cdots,\lambda_{l})
where
\lambda_{1}\geq\lambda_{2}\geq\cdots\geq\lambda_{l}>0
be a partition of
n
. Every partition corresponds to a Young diagram, and in the following paragraph, we will use
\lambda
to denote both the partition and its Young diagram. For
\lambda=(\lambda_{1},\cdots,\lambda_{l})
and
\mu=(\mu_{1},\cdots,\mu_{l^{\prime}})
, we define
\lambda\cup\mu
to be the partition obtained by reordering the tuple
(\lambda_{1},\cdots,\lambda_{l},\mu_{1},\cdots,\mu_{l^{\prime}})
.

For a box
a
in a partition
\lambda
, we define the
hook length
by

\mathrm{hk}(a)=\#\{\text{boxes below }a\}+\#\{\text{boxes to the right of }a\}+1.

The
2-core
of
\lambda
is defined as the boxes of
\lambda
with
\mathrm{hk}(a)\neq 2
. We denote by
\tilde{\lambda}
the 2-core of
\lambda
. We have the fact that every 2-core has the shape of a staircase
\delta_{r}=(r,r-1,\cdots,0)
for some integer
r\geq 0
. For two partitions
\mu\subset\lambda
we denote by
\lambda/\mu
to be the skew partition obtained by the set-difference of the diagrams of
\lambda
and
\mu
.

A
domino tableaux

T
of shape
\mathrm{sh}(T)=\lambda
with even
|\lambda/\tilde{\lambda}|
consists of a tiling of the shape
\lambda/\tilde{\lambda}
by dominoes and a filling of each domino with an integer. It is called
standard
if the filling is by integers in
[n]
where
n=\frac{1}{2}|\lambda/\tilde{\lambda}|
and the numbers in the filling are increasing reading along the rows and columns. A
domino
is the shape given by two adjacent squares sharing a common edge, i.e.,
1\times 2
or
2\times 1
rectangles. The
label
of a domino is the number filling into it. We will write
\mathsf{dom}_{i}
to indicate the domino with label
i
.

We denote by
\mathrm{SDT}^{r}(n)
the set of all standard domino tableaux of
n
dominoes with core of shape
\delta_{r}
. We call the elements of
\mathrm{SDT}^{r}(n)
the
standard
r
-domino tableaux
. For a domino tableau
T
, we define its label set to be
{\mathrm{label}}(T)=\{i|\mathsf{dom}_{i}\in T\}
.

Example 3.1
.

For example the following is a
2
-domino tableau in

\mathrm{SDT}^{2}(5)
.

3.2
Domino insertion

Garfinkle
[
9
, Theorem 1.2.13]
provides an algorithm by which any signed
permutation
w\in W^{\mathsf{BC}}_{n}
is assigned bijectively to a pair of
same shape standard
r
-domino tableau
(P^{r}(w),Q^{r}(w))

for
r=0,1
, where
P_{\mathsf{D}}^{r}(w)
is called the
insertion tableau
and

Q_{\mathsf{D}}^{r}(w)
is called the
recording tableau
of
w
. Her
algorithm is extended by van Leeuwen
[
15
]
for larger
cores.

In the following we will explain how to insert an integer into a

r
-domino tableau according to Garfinkle’s algorithm. Here we follow the description in Lam
[
14
]
with a few modification.
Let
T
be
a
r
-domino tableau such that
i=|a|\not\in{\mathrm{label}}(T)
. We denote by

T\xleftarrow{\hskip 1.42262pt\mathsf{D}\hskip 1.42262pt}a

the tableau which is obtained by inserting
a
into
T
.

We set
B
to be the domino tableaux containing the sub-domino tableaux containing dominoes with labels less than
i
and an additional vertical domino labelled
i
in the first column if
a<0
or an additional horizontal domino labelled
i
in the first row if
a>0
. Let
C
be the skew domino tableaux containing dominoes with labels greater than
i
. Now the bumping procedure is given recursively as follows.

Let
(B,C)
be a pair of domino tableaux (with no values repeated) overlapping in at most a domino which contains the label
i
of dominoes in
B
. Let
j<i
be the smallest label of dominoes in
C
. Denote the corresponding dominoes by
\gamma_{i}
and
\gamma_{j}
. We now distinguish four cases:

(1)

If
\gamma_{i}
and
\gamma_{j}
do not overlap, then we set
B^{\prime}=B\cup\gamma_{j}
.

(2)

If
B
and
\gamma_{j}
intersect in exactly one box, then we set
B^{\prime}
to be the domino tableaux with shape
B\cup\gamma
where
\gamma
is a domino containing the intersecting box and an additional box below it or to the right of it, where
\gamma
is vertical if
\gamma_{j}
is horizontal and vice versa. To fill
B^{\prime}
, we just label the new domino
\gamma
by
j
.

(3)

If
B
and
\gamma_{j}
intersect in the domino
\gamma_{j}
and
\gamma_{j}
is horizontal, then we set
B^{\prime}
to be the domino tableaux containing
B
with an additional horizontal domino labeled
j
one row below that of the original position of
\gamma_{j}
in
C
.

(4)

If
B
and
\gamma_{j}
intersect in the domino
\gamma_{j}
and
\gamma_{j}
is vertical, then we set
B^{\prime}
to be the domino tableaux containing
B
with an additional vertical domino labeled
j
one column to the right of the original position of
\gamma_{j}
in
C
.

Then replace
(B,C)
by
(B^{\prime},C-\gamma_{j})
and do the process recursively until the
C=\varnothing
.
The resulting
B
tableaux will be denoted by
T\xleftarrow{\hskip 1.42262pt\mathsf{D}\hskip 1.42262pt}a
.

Then we define the insertion process of
w=w_{1}\cdots w_{n}
as follows:
Let
P_{0}
and
Q_{0}
to be the tableaux of shape
\delta_{r}=(r,\cdots,2,1)
whose cells are all filed with
0
.
For
w=w_{1}\cdots w_{n}\in W^{\mathsf{BC}}_{n}
let
P_{i+1}=P_{i}\xleftarrow{\hskip 1.42262pt\mathsf{D}\hskip 1.42262pt}w_{i}
and let
Q_{i+1}
be the domino tableaux with same shape as
P_{i+1}
, obtained from
Q_{i}
by labeling the newly appearing domino
P_{i+1}-P_{i}
by
i+1
in
Q_{i+1}
. Then one can obtain
P^{r}(w)
and
Q^{r}(w)
by erasing all zeros of
P_{n}
and
Q_{n}
.

Example 3.2
.

Consider
T\xleftarrow{\hskip 1.42262pt\mathsf{D}\hskip 1.42262pt}\overline{2}
for

3.3
Vogan’s map

In this section, we follows Taskin
[
25
]
to define the Knuth relation similar to the one in the symmetric group. Moreover, we study the effect of Knuth relations on the insertion tableaux and recording tableaux.

Recall that the left descents of
w
are defined as
\mathrm{Des}_{L}(w):=\{i\in[0,n-1]|\ell(s_{i}w)<\ell(w)\}
. Now we define the descent set of a
r
-domino tableau
T
:

\mathrm{Des}(T):=\left\{i|\text{ if }\mathsf{dom}_{i+1}\text{ lies below }\mathsf{dom}_{i}\right\}\cup\{0|\text{ if }\mathsf{dom}_{1}\text{ is vertical}\}

It is a well known property of the RSK algorithm that for a
permutation
w\in S_{n}
, we have
\mathrm{Des}_{L}(w)=\mathrm{Des}(P_{\mathsf{RSK}}(w))
where
\mathrm{Des}(T)=\{i\mid i+1~\text{lies below}~i~\text{in}~T\}
. Now in
W^{\mathsf{BC}}_{n}
, similarly we have:

Proposition 3.3

(
[
25
, Proposition 2.26]
)
.

For
w\in W^{\mathsf{BC}}_{n}
we have
\mathrm{Des}_{L}(w)=\mathrm{Des}(P_{\mathsf{D}}^{r}(w))
.

Suppose
a<b<c
are integers.
acb
,
bac
,
bca
, and
cab
are the only four permutations not strictly increasing or strictly decreasing. A
Knuth move
on these words exchanges the
a
and
c
letters. Thus
acb
and
cab
are connected by a Knuth move.

Suppose
v,w\in S_{n}
or
v,w\in W^{\mathsf{BC}}_{n}
and
i
is an integer with
1<i<n
.
We write
v\mathbin{\overset{i}{\underset{\mathsf{K}}{\scalebox{1.5}[1]{$\sim$}}}}w
and say that
v
and
w
are connected by a
Knuth move
if
w
is obtained from
v
by performing a Knuth move on
v_{i-1}v_{i}v_{i+1}
, or if
v=w
when the subword
v_{i-1}v_{i}v_{i+1}
is in monotonic order.
v,w
are called
Knuth equivalent
if they are connected by a sequence of Knuth moves.

It can be shown that Knuth equivalent
w,v
yields
P_{\mathsf{D}}^{r}(w)=P_{\mathsf{D}}^{r}(v)
. For the relations between
Q_{\mathsf{D}}(w)
and
Q_{\mathsf{D}}(v)
, Garfinkle
[
10
, 2.1.10–2.1.19]
studied the case when
r=0,1
and Taskin
[
25
]
studied the other cases.

Let
r\geq 0
. For
i,j
two adjacent integers satisfying
1\leq i,j\leq n-1
, consider the following sets:

\displaystyle D_{i,j}(W^{\mathsf{BC}}_{n})

\displaystyle:=\{w\in W^{\mathsf{BC}}_{n}|i\in\mathrm{Des}_{L}(w)\text{ but }j\not\in\mathrm{Des}_{L}(w)\}

\displaystyle D_{i,j}(\mathrm{SDT}^{r}(n))

\displaystyle:=\{T\in\mathrm{SDT}^{r}(n)|i\in\mathrm{Des}(T)\text{ but }j\not\in\mathrm{Des}(T)\}.

Moreover, we define the Vogan’s map
V_{i,j}:D_{i,j}(W^{\mathsf{BC}}_{n})\to D_{j,i}(W^{\mathsf{BC}}_{n})
where
V_{i,j}(w)=\{s_{i}w,s_{j}w\}\cap D_{j,i}(W^{\mathsf{BC}}_{n}).

Also, we define the Vogan’s map on tableau

V_{i,j}:D_{i,j}(\mathrm{SDT}^{r}(n))\to D_{j,i}(\mathrm{SDT}^{r}(n))

in the following way:

Without loss of generality we assume that
j=i+1
.
Observe that if
i\in\mathrm{Des}(T)
but
i+1\not\in\mathrm{Des}(T)
then
\mathsf{dom}_{i+1}

lies below
\mathsf{dom}_{i}
in
T
whereas
\mathsf{dom}_{i+2}
lies right to
\mathsf{dom}_{i+1}
in
T
.
On the other hand we have two cases according to the positions of
\mathsf{dom}_{i}
and
\mathsf{dom}_{i+2}
with respect to each other.

Case 1
. We first assume that
\mathsf{dom}_{i+2}
lies below
\mathsf{dom}_{i}
in
T
. Since the
\mathsf{dom}_{i+2}
lies to the right of
\mathsf{dom}_{i+1}
and
\mathsf{dom}_{i+1}
lies below
\mathsf{dom}_{i}
we have two cases to consider:
If the boundaries of
\mathsf{dom}_{i+1}
and
\mathsf{dom}_{i}
intersect at most at a point then
V_{i,i+1}(T)
is obtained by interchanging the labels
i
and
i+1
in
T
.
Otherwise there is only one possibility which satisfies
\mathsf{dom}_{i+2}
lies below
\mathsf{dom}_{i}
and it lies to the right of
\mathsf{dom}_{i+1}
, in which
T
has the subtableau
U
as illustrated below and
V_{i,i+1}(T)
is obtained by
substituting
U
with
U^{\prime}
in
T
.

. Now we assume
\mathsf{dom}_{i+2}
lies strictly right to

\mathsf{dom}_{i}
in

. Again if the boundaries of

and

.

0.0pt}\end{array}}

These maps are named after Vogan because they were first introduced on the symmetric group by him
[
26
]
. He defined them to help classify the primitive ideals in the universal enveloping algebra of complex semisimple Lie algebras.

Example 3.4
.

We have
T_{2}=V_{5,6}(T_{1})
,
T_{3}=V_{3,4}(T_{2})
, and

T_{4}=V_{4,5}(T_{3})=V_{6,5}(T_{3})
for the following tableaux. Here we bolden the label
i,i+1,i+2
in each step.

Lemma 3.5

(
[
25
, Lemma 2.31]
)
.

Let
i
and
j
be two consecutive integers such that
1\leq i,j\leq n-1
. Suppose
w\in D_{i,j}(W^{\mathsf{BC}}_{n})
. Then
P_{\mathsf{D}}^{r}(w)\in D_{i,j}(\mathrm{SDT}^{r}(n))
and

P_{\mathsf{D}}^{r}(V_{i,j}(w))=V_{i,j}(P_{\mathsf{D}}^{r}(w)).

Proof.

This result is first proven by Garfinkle
[
10
, Theorem 2.1.19]
for
r=0,1
. But his proofs can easily be extended to all valid
r
.
∎

Corollary 3.6

(
[
25
, Corollary 2.32]
)
.

Suppose
w=w_{1}\cdots w_{i-1}(w_{i}w_{i+1})w_{i+2}\cdots w_{n}
and
\beta=w_{1}\cdots w_{i-1}(w_{i+1}w_{i})w_{i+2}\cdots w_{n}
differ by a single
Knuth relation. Then one of the following is satisfied:

1)

w_{i}<w_{i+2}<w_{i+1}
then
\beta^{-1}=V_{i+1,i}(w^{-1})
,
Q_{\mathsf{D}}^{r}(\beta)=V_{i+1,i}(Q_{\mathsf{D}}^{r}(w))
.

2)

w_{i}>w_{i+2}>w_{i+1}
then
\beta^{-1}=V_{i,i+1}(w^{-1})
,
Q_{\mathsf{D}}^{r}(\beta)=V_{i,i+1}(Q_{\mathsf{D}}^{r}(w))
.

3)

w_{i}<w_{i-1}<w_{i+1}
then
\beta^{-1}=V_{i-1,i}(w^{-1})
,
Q_{\mathsf{D}}^{r}(\beta)=V_{i-1,i}(Q_{\mathsf{D}}^{r}(w))
.

4)

w_{i}>w_{i-1}>w_{i+1}
then

\beta^{-1}=V_{i,i-1}(w^{-1})
,
Q_{\mathsf{D}}^{r}(\beta)=V_{i,i-1}(Q_{\mathsf{D}}^{r}(w))
.

3.4
Plactic relation

In this section, we define the plactic relations and show that they preserves the insertion tableaux following Taskin
[
25
]
. Then we study the effect of plactic relations on recording tableaux.

Definition 3.7

(
[
25
, Definition 3.1]
)
.

For two signed permutations
w
,
v
in
W^{\mathsf{BC}}_{n}
and

r\geq 0
, we say
w
and
v
are
r
-plactic
equivalent
,
w{\overset{p_{r}}{\sim}}v
, if one of them can be obtained
from the other by applying a sequence of
\mathrm{D}_{i}^{r}
relations
for
i=1,\cdots 5
, explained below. Moreover, we say
w
and

v
are
r
-coplactic equivalent
,
w~{\overset{p_{r}^{*}}{\sim}}~v
, if

w^{-1}{\overset{p_{r}}{\sim}}v^{-1}
.

Let
w=w_{1}\cdots w_{m}\in W^{\mathsf{BC}}_{n}
.

\mathrm{D}_{1}^{r}
:

If
w_{i}<w_{i+2}<w_{i+1}
or
w_{i}<w_{i-1}<w_{i+1}

for some
i\leq m-1
, then

w=w_{1}\cdots w_{i-1}(w_{i}w_{i+1})w_{i+2}\cdots w_{m}\sim w_{1}\cdots w_{i-1}(w_{i+1}w_{i})w_{i+2}\cdots w_{m}

\mathrm{D}_{2}^{r}
:

If
r\geq 1
and if there exists
0<j\leq r
such that
w_{j}
and
w_{j+1}
have opposite signs then

w=w_{1}\cdots(w_{j}w_{j+1})\cdots w_{r+2}\cdots w_{m}\sim w_{1}\cdots(w_{j+1}w_{j})\cdots w_{r+2}\cdots w_{m}

\mathrm{D}_{3}^{r}
:

Suppose that
|w_{1}|>|w_{i}|
for all
2\leq i\leq r+2

and
w_{2}\cdots w_{r+2}
is obtained by concatenating
some positive decreasing sequence to the end of some negative
increasing sequence (or vice versa), where at least one of the
sequences is nonempty. Then

w=w_{1}w_{2}\cdots w_{r+2}\cdots w_{m}\sim\overline{w_{1}}w_{2}\cdots w_{r+2}\cdots w_{m}

\mathrm{D}_{4}^{r}
:

Let
for some
k\geq 1
,
s=(k+1)(r+k+1)\leq m
and
u=w_{1}\cdots w_{s-1}
is obtained by concatenating the sequences

a_{i,i+r}\cdots a_{i,1}
and
b_{i,i}\cdots b_{i,1}
for
1\leq i\leq k
and
a_{k+1,r+k}\cdots a_{1,1}
in the following way:

u=w_{1}\cdots w_{s-1}=a_{{}_{1,r+1}}\cdots a_{{}_{1,1}}b_{{}_{1,1}}\cdots a_{{}_{k,r+k}}\cdots a_{{}_{k,1}}b_{{}_{k,k}}\cdots b_{{}_{k,1}}a_{{}_{k+1,r+k}}\cdots a_{{}_{k+1,1}}

where the integers
a_{{}_{i,j}}
and
b_{{}_{i,j}}
, if exist in
u
,
satisfy the following conditions:

\displaystyle a_{{}_{i,j}}>0~\mbox{ and }~b_{{}_{i,j}}<0~\mbox{(or vice versa)}

\displaystyle|a_{{}_{i,j-1}}|<|a_{{}_{i,j}}|<|a_{{}_{i+1,j}}|~\mbox{ and }~|b_{{}_{i,j-1}}|<|b_{{}_{i,j}}|<|b_{{}_{i+1,j}}|

\displaystyle|b_{{}_{i,i}}|<|a_{{}_{i+1,r+i+1}}|<|b_{{}_{i+1,i+1}}|~\mbox{for all
}~i=1,\cdots,k-1.

Let
n=\mathrm{max}\{|w_{1}|,\cdots,|w_{s-1}|\}
and
suppose that the integer
w_{s}=z
satisfies one of the
followings:

i.

|b_{{}_{k,k}}|=n
and
z
is an integer between
a_{{}_{k+1,1}}
and

b_{{}_{k,1}}

ii.

|a_{{}_{k+1,r+k}}|=n
and
z
is an integer between

a_{{}_{k,1}}
and
b_{{}_{k,1}}

iii.

|a_{{}_{k+1,r+k}}|=n
,
z
is an integer between

a_{{}_{k,1}}
and
a_{{}_{k+1,1}}
and
|a_{{}_{k+1,i}}|<|a_{{}_{k,i+1}}|

Then we set

w=uzw_{s+1}\cdots w_{m}~\sim~u^{\prime}zw_{s+1}\cdots w_{m}

where

u^{\prime}=a_{{}_{1,r+1}}\cdots a_{{}_{1,1}}b_{{}_{1,1}}\cdots\overline{b_{{}_{k,k}}}a_{{}_{k,r+k}}\cdots a_{{}_{k,1}}b_{{}_{k,k-1}}\cdots b_{{}_{k,1}}a_{{}_{k+1,r+k}}\cdots a_{{}_{k+1,1}}
.

\mathrm{D}_{5}^{r}
:

Let
for some
k\geq 1
,
s=(k+1)(r+k+2)\leq m
and
u=w_{1}\cdots w_{s-1}
is
obtained by concatenating the sequences
a_{i,i+r}\cdots a_{i,1}
and
b_{i,i}\cdots b_{i,1}
for
1\leq i\leq k
and
a_{k+1,r+k+1}\cdots a_{1,1}
and
b_{k+k,k}\cdots a_{1,1}
in the following way:

u=w_{1}\cdots w_{s-1}=a_{{}_{1,r+1}}\cdots a_{{}_{1,1}}b_{{}_{1,1}}\cdots a_{{}_{k,r+k}}\cdots a_{{}_{k,1}}b_{{}_{k,k}}\cdots b_{{}_{k,1}}a_{{}_{k+1,r+k+1}}\cdots a_{{}_{k+1,1}}b_{{}_{k+1,k}}\cdots b_{{}_{k+1,1}}

where the integers
a_{{}_{i,j}}
and
b_{{}_{i,j}}
, if exist in
u
,
satisfy the followings:

\displaystyle a_{{}_{i,j}}>0~\mbox{ and }~b_{{}_{i,j}}<0~\mbox{(or vice versa)}

\displaystyle|a_{{}_{i,j-1}}|<|a_{{}_{i,j}}|<|a_{{}_{i+1,j}}|~\mbox{ and }~|b_{{}_{i,j-1}}|<|b_{{}_{i,j}}|<|b_{{}_{i+1,j}}|

\displaystyle|a_{{}_{i,r+i}}|<|b_{{}_{i,i}}|<|a_{{}_{i+1,r+i+1}}|~\mbox{for all
}~i=1,\cdots,k.

Let
n=\mathrm{max}\{|w_{1}|,\cdots,|w_{s-1}|\}
and
suppose that the integer
w_{s}=z
satisfies one of the
followings:

i.

|a_{{}_{k+1,r+k+1}}|=n
and
z
is an integer between

a_{{}_{k+1,1}}
and
b_{{}_{k+1,1}}

ii.

|b_{{}_{k+1,k}}|=n
and
z
is an integer between

a_{{}_{k+1,1}}
and
b_{{}_{k,1}}

iii.

|b_{{}_{k+1,k}}|=n
,
z
is an integer between

b_{{}_{k,1}}
and
b_{{}_{k+1,1}}
and
|b_{{}_{k+1,i}}|<|b_{{}_{k,i+1}}|

for some
1<i\leq k-1
.

Then we set

w=uzw_{s+1}\cdots w_{m}~\sim~u^{\prime}zw_{s+1}\cdots w_{m}

where

u^{\prime}=a_{1,r+1}\cdots a_{{}_{1,1}}b_{{}_{1,1}}\cdots a_{{}_{k,r+k}}\cdots a_{{}_{k,1}}\overline{a_{{}_{k+1,r+k+1}}}b_{{}_{k,k}}\cdots b_{{}_{k,1}}a_{{}_{k+1,r+k}}\cdots a_{{}_{k+1,1}}b_{{}_{k+1,k}}\cdots b_{{}_{k+1,1}}
.

Theorem 3.8

(
[
25
, Theorem 3.4]
)
.

For
w,v\in W^{\mathsf{BC}}_{n}
,
w~{\overset{p_{r}}{\sim}}~v
if and only if they have the same insertion
r
-domino tableaux.

Taskin showed that the plactic relation preserves the insertion tableaux. So what about the recording tableaux? We will show the results below.

Proposition 3.9
.

For two signed permutations
w
,
v
in
W^{\mathsf{BC}}_{n}
and

r\geq 0
, if they are related by a
\mathrm{D}_{2}^{r}
relation, say
w=w_{1}\cdots(w_{j}w_{j+1})\cdots w_{r+2}\cdots w_{m}\sim w_{1}\cdots(w_{j+1}w_{j})\cdots w_{r+2}\cdots w_{m}=v
, then
Q_{\mathsf{D}}^{r}(v)
is obtained from
Q_{\mathsf{D}}^{r}(w)
by interchanging the labels
j
and
j+1
.

Proof.

Consider a word
w^{\prime}=w_{1}w_{2}\cdots w_{r}
. Define
a_{1}<\cdots<a_{p}
and
b_{1}<\cdots<b_{q}
with
\{a_{1},\cdots,a_{p},b_{1},\cdots,b_{q}\}=[r]
,
w_{a_{1}},\cdots,w_{a_{p}}>0
and
w_{b_{1}},\cdots,w_{b_{q}}<0
. Then we claim that in the insertion algorithm of
w^{\prime}
, the insertions of
w_{a_{i}}
and
w_{b_{i^{\prime}}}
do not affect each other.

To prove this claim, it suffices to show that in
P_{\mathsf{D}}(w^{\prime})
,
\mathsf{dom}_{w_{a_{i}}}
and
\mathsf{dom}_{w_{b_{i^{\prime}}}}
do not appear in a same row or column. We show this by contradiction. Assume we have
\mathsf{dom}_{w_{a_{i}}}
and
\mathsf{dom}_{w_{b_{i^{\prime}}}}
appear in the same row, say row
k
, and we assume
a_{i}
and
b_{i^{\prime}}
to be the smallest. Then we must have
i\geq k
and
i^{\prime}\geq r+1-k
since the core is
(r,r-1,\cdots,2,1)
. Thus we have
i+i^{\prime}\geq r+1
which contradicts the fact that
i+i^{\prime}\leq p+q=r
.
∎

14.39996pt\hskip-0.2pt\rule[-3.60004pt]{0.4pt}{14.39996pt}\hskip-0.2pt\hskip-14.39996pt\hskip-0.2pt\rule[-3.80003pt]{0.2pt}{0.4pt}\rule[-3.80003pt]{14.39996pt}{0.4pt}\rule[-3.80003pt]{0.2pt}{0.4pt}\hskip-14.39996pt\hskip-0.2pt}{}}}{\makebox[14.39996pt][c]{${}$}}}$}}}\phantom{\rule[-3.60004pt]{14.39996pt}{14.39996pt}}\raisebox{0.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\makebox[0.0pt][l]{\makebox[14.39996pt][c]{${\raisebox{0.0pt}{\mbox{{}{\hskip-0.2pt\rule[-3.60004pt]{0.4pt}{14.39996pt}\hskip-0.2pt\hskip 14.39996pt\hskip-0.2pt\rule[-3.60004pt]{0.4pt}{14.39996pt}\hskip-0.2pt\hskip-14.39996pt\hskip-0.2pt\rule[-3.80003pt]{0.2pt}{0.4pt}\rule[-3.80003pt]{14.39996pt}{0.4pt}\rule[-3.80003pt]{0.2pt}{0.4pt}\hskip-14.39996pt\hskip-0.2pt}{}}}{\makebox[14.39996pt][c]{${}$}}}$}}}\phantom{\rule[-3.60004pt]{14.39996pt}{14.39996pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt}\end{array}}\Rightarrow\cdots

with the recording process:

w:\varnothing\Rightarrow\mathrel{\begin{array}[]{@{\,}c@{\,}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\makebox[0.0pt][l]{\makebox[14.39996pt][c]{${\raisebox{0.0pt}{\mbox{{}{}{\hskip-0.2pt\rule[-3.60004pt]{0.4pt}{14.39996pt}\hskip-0.2pt\hskip-0.2pt\rule[10.59993pt]{0.2pt}{0.4pt}\rule[10.59993pt]{14.39996pt}{0.4pt}\rule[10.59993pt]{0.2pt}{0.4pt}\hskip-14.39996pt\hskip-0.2pt\hskip-0.2pt\rule[-3.80003pt]{0.2pt}{0.4pt}\rule[-3.80003pt]{14.39996pt}{0.4pt}\rule[-3.80003pt]{0.2pt}{0.4pt}\hskip-14.39996pt\hskip-0.2pt}{}}}{\makebox[14.39996pt][c]{${1}$}}}$}}}\phantom{\rule[-3.60004pt]{14.39996pt}{14.39996pt}}\raisebox{0.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\makebox[0.0pt][l]{\makebox[14.39996pt][c]{${\raisebox{0.0pt}{\mbox{{}{\hskip 14.39996pt\hskip-0.2pt\rule[-3.60004pt]{0.4pt}{14.39996pt}\hskip-0.2pt\hskip-14.39996pt\hskip-0.2pt\rule[10.59993pt]{0.2pt}{0.4pt}\rule[10.59993pt]{14.39996pt}{0.4pt}\rule[10.59993pt]{0.2pt}{0.4pt}\hskip-14.39996pt\hskip-0.2pt\hskip-0.2pt\rule[-3.80003pt]{0.2pt}{0.4pt}\rule[-3.80003pt]{14.39996pt}{0.4pt}\rule[-3.80003pt]{0.2pt}{0.4pt}\hskip-14.39996pt\hskip-0.2pt}{}}}{\makebox[14.39996pt][c]{${}$}}}$}}}\phantom{\rule[-3.60004pt]{14.39996pt}{14.39996pt}}}\end{array}}\Rightarrow\mathrel{\begin{array}[]{@{\,}c@{\,}}{\raisebox{6.44444pt}{\makebox[0.0pt][l]{{\raisebox{0.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\makebox[0.0pt][l]{\makebox[14.39996pt][c]{${\raisebox{0.0pt}{\mbox{{}{}{\hskip-0.2pt\rule[-3.60004pt]{0.4pt}{14.39996pt}\hskip-0.2pt\hskip-0.2pt\rule[10.59993pt]{0.2pt}{0.4pt}\rule[10.59993pt]{14.39996pt}{0.4pt}\rule[10.59993pt]{0.2pt}{0.4pt}\hskip-14.39996pt\hskip-0.2pt\hskip-0.2pt\rule[-3.80003pt]{0.2pt}{0.4pt}\rule[-3.80003pt]{14.39996pt}{0.4pt}\rule[-3.80003pt]{0.2pt}{0.4pt}\hskip-14.39996pt\hskip-0.2pt}{}}}{\makebox[14.39996pt][c]{${1}$}}}$}}}\phantom{\rule[-3.60004pt]{14.39996pt}{14.39996pt}}\raisebox{0.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\makebox[0.0pt][l]{\makebox[14.39996pt][c]{${\raisebox{0.0pt}{\mbox{{}{\hskip 14.39996pt\hskip-0.2pt\rule[-3.60004pt]{0.4pt}{14.39996pt}\hskip-0.2pt\hskip-14.39996pt\hskip-0.2pt\rule[10.59993pt]{0.2pt}{0.4pt}\rule[10.59993pt]{14.39996pt}{0.4pt}\rule[10.59993pt]{0.2pt}{0.4pt}\hskip-14.39996pt\hskip-0.2pt\hskip-0.2pt\rule[-3.80003pt]{0.2pt}{0.4pt}\rule[-3.80003pt]{14.39996pt}{0.4pt}\rule[-3.80003pt]{0.2pt}{0.4pt}\hskip-14.39996pt\hskip-0.2pt}{}}}{\makebox[14.39996pt][c]{${}$}}}$}}}\phantom{\rule[-3.60004pt]{14.39996pt}{14.39996pt}}}}}\makebox[0.0pt][l]{\raisebox{0.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\makebox[0.0pt][l]{\makebox[14.39996pt][c]{${\raisebox{0.0pt}{\mbox{{}{}{\hskip-0.2pt\rule[-3.60004pt]{0.4pt}{14.39996pt}\hskip-0.2pt\hskip-0.2pt\rule[10.59993pt]{0.2pt}{0.4pt}\rule[10.59993pt]{14.39996pt}{0.4pt}\rule[10.59993pt]{0.2pt}{0.4pt}\hskip-14.39996pt\hskip-0.2pt\hskip-0.2pt\rule[-3.80003pt]{0.2pt}{0.4pt}\rule[-3.80003pt]{14.39996pt}{0.4pt}\rule[-3.80003pt]{0.2pt}{0.4pt}\hskip-14.39996pt\hskip-0.2pt}{}}}{\makebox[14.39996pt][c]{${2}$}}}$}}}\phantom{\rule[-3.60004pt]{14.39996pt}{14.39996pt}}\raisebox{0.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\makebox[0.0pt][l]{\makebox[14.39996pt][c]{${\raisebox{0.0pt}{\mbox{{}{\hskip 14.39996pt\hskip-0.2pt\rule[-3.60004pt]{0.4pt}{14.39996pt}\hskip-0.2pt\hskip-14.39996pt\hskip-0.2pt\rule[10.59993pt]{0.2pt}{0.4pt}\rule[10.59993pt]{14.39996pt}{0.4pt}\rule[10.59993pt]{0.2pt}{0.4pt}\hskip-14.39996pt\hskip-0.2pt\hskip-0.2pt\rule[-3.80003pt]{0.2pt}{0.4pt}\rule[-3.80003pt]{14.39996pt}{0.4pt}\rule[-3.80003pt]{0.2pt}{0.4pt}\hskip-14.39996pt\hskip-0.2pt}{}}}{\makebox[14.39996pt][c]{${}$}}}$}}}\phantom{\rule[-3.60004pt]{14.39996pt}{14.39996pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt}\end{array}}\Rightarrow\cdots\qquad v:\varnothing\Rightarrow\mathrel{\begin{array}[]{@{\,}c@{\,}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\makebox[0.0pt][l]{{\raisebox{0.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\makebox[0.0pt][l]{\makebox[14.39996pt][c]{${\raisebox{0.0pt}{\mbox{{}{}{\hskip-0.2pt\rule[-3.60004pt]{0.4pt}{14.39996pt}\hskip-0.2pt\hskip 14.39996pt\hskip-0.2pt\rule[-3.60004pt]{0.4pt}{14.39996pt}\hskip-0.2pt\hskip-14.39996pt\hskip-0.2pt\rule[10.59993pt]{0.2pt}{0.4pt}\rule[10.59993pt]{14.39996pt}{0.4pt}\rule[10.59993pt]{0.2pt}{0.4pt}\hskip-14.39996pt\hskip-0.2pt}{}}}{\makebox[14.39996pt][c]{${1}$}}}$}}}\phantom{\rule[-3.60004pt]{14.39996pt}{14.39996pt}}}}}\makebox[0.0pt][l]{\raisebox{0.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\makebox[0.0pt][l]{\makebox[14.39996pt][c]{${\raisebox{0.0pt}{\mbox{{}{}{\hskip-0.2pt\rule[-3.60004pt]{0.4pt}{14.39996pt}\hskip-0.2pt\hskip 14.39996pt\hskip-0.2pt\rule[-3.60004pt]{0.4pt}{14.39996pt}\hskip-0.2pt\hskip-14.39996pt\hskip-0.2pt\rule[-3.80003pt]{0.2pt}{0.4pt}\rule[-3.80003pt]{14.39996pt}{0.4pt}\rule[-3.80003pt]{0.2pt}{0.4pt}\hskip-14.39996pt\hskip-0.2pt}{}}}{\makebox[14.39996pt][c]{${}$}}}$}}}\phantom{\rule[-3.60004pt]{14.39996pt}{14.39996pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt}\end{array}}\Rightarrow\mathrel{\begin{array}[]{@{\,}c@{\,}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\makebox[0.0pt][l]{{\raisebox{0.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\makebox[0.0pt][l]{\makebox[14.39996pt][c]{${\raisebox{0.0pt}{\mbox{{}{}{\hskip-0.2pt\rule[-3.60004pt]{0.4pt}{14.39996pt}\hskip-0.2pt\hskip 14.39996pt\hskip-0.2pt\rule[-3.60004pt]{0.4pt}{14.39996pt}\hskip-0.2pt\hskip-14.39996pt\hskip-0.2pt\rule[10.59993pt]{0.2pt}{0.4pt}\rule[10.59993pt]{14.39996pt}{0.4pt}\rule[10.59993pt]{0.2pt}{0.4pt}\hskip-14.39996pt\hskip-0.2pt}{}}}{\makebox[14.39996pt][c]{${1}$}}}$}}}\phantom{\rule[-3.60004pt]{14.39996pt}{14.39996pt}}\raisebox{0.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\makebox[0.0pt][l]{\makebox[14.39996pt][c]{${\raisebox{0.0pt}{\mbox{{}{\hskip-0.2pt\rule[-3.60004pt]{0.4pt}{14.39996pt}\hskip-0.2pt\hskip 14.39996pt\hskip-0.2pt\rule[-3.60004pt]{0.4pt}{14.39996pt}\hskip-0.2pt\hskip-14.39996pt\hskip-0.2pt\rule[10.59993pt]{0.2pt}{0.4pt}\rule[10.59993pt]{14.39996pt}{0.4pt}\rule[10.59993pt]{0.2pt}{0.4pt}\hskip-14.39996pt\hskip-0.2pt}{}}}{\makebox[14.39996pt][c]{${2}$}}}$}}}\phantom{\rule[-3.60004pt]{14.39996pt}{14.39996pt}}}}}\makebox[0.0pt][l]{\raisebox{0.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\makebox[0.0pt][l]{\makebox[14.39996pt][c]{${\raisebox{0.0pt}{\mbox{{}{}{\hskip-0.2pt\rule[-3.60004pt]{0.4pt}{14.39996pt}\hskip-0.2pt\hskip 14.39996pt\hskip-0.2pt\rule[-3.60004pt]{0.4pt}{14.39996pt}\hskip-0.2pt\hskip-14.39996pt\hskip-0.2pt\rule[-3.80003pt]{0.2pt}{0.4pt}\rule[-3.80003pt]{14.39996pt}{0.4pt}\rule[-3.80003pt]{0.2pt}{0.4pt}\hskip-14.39996pt\hskip-0.2pt}{}}}{\makebox[14.39996pt][c]{${}$}}}$}}}\phantom{\rule[-3.60004pt]{14.39996pt}{14.39996pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt}\end{array}}\Rightarrow\cdots

Since the insertion tableau after inserting
w_{2}
are same for
w
and
v
, we get the required result.

For the cases when
w_{1}<0
, consider
w^{\prime}=v
and
v^{\prime}=w
, then it comes back to the case discussed above.
∎

Theorem 3.11
.

For two signed permutations
w
,
v
in
W^{\mathsf{BC}}_{n}
, if they are related by a
\mathrm{D}_{3}^{r}
relation, say
w=w_{1}w_{2}\cdots w_{r+2}\cdots w_{m}\sim\overline{w_{1}}w_{2}\cdots w_{r+2}\cdots w_{m}=v
. Define
w^{\prime}=w_{1}w_{2}\cdots w_{r+2}
,
v^{\prime}=\overline{w_{1}}w_{2}\cdots w_{r+2}
and
u=u_{1}\cdots u_{r+2}\in\{w^{\prime},v^{\prime}\}
, then we have the following properties:

1.

Q_{\mathsf{D}}^{r}(w^{\prime})
and
Q_{\mathsf{D}}^{r}(v^{\prime})
have the same shape
(r+2,r+1,\cdots,2,1)/\penalty(r,r-1,\cdots,2,1)
plus an extra box
{\mathrel{\begin{array}[]{@{\,}c@{\,}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\makebox[0.0pt][l]{\makebox[7.20007pt][c]{${\raisebox{0.0pt}{\mbox{{\hskip-0.2pt\rule[5.20006pt]{0.2pt}{0.4pt}\rule[5.20006pt]{7.20007pt}{0.4pt}\rule[5.20006pt]{0.2pt}{0.4pt}\hskip-7.20007pt\hskip-0.2pt}{\hskip-0.2pt\rule[-2.00002pt]{0.2pt}{0.4pt}\rule[-2.00002pt]{7.20007pt}{0.4pt}\rule[-2.00002pt]{0.2pt}{0.4pt}\hskip-7.20007pt\hskip-0.2pt}{\hskip-0.2pt\rule[-1.80002pt]{0.4pt}{7.20007pt}\hskip-0.2pt}{\hskip 7.20007pt\hskip-0.2pt\rule[-1.80002pt]{0.4pt}{7.20007pt}\hskip-0.2pt\hskip-7.20007pt}{}}}{\makebox[7.20007pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle{}$}}}$}}}\phantom{\rule[-1.80002pt]{7.20007pt}{7.20007pt}}}\end{array}}}
. This extra box does not appear in the first row and first column.

2.

In
Q_{\mathsf{D}}^{r}(u)
, the extra box
{\mathrel{\begin{array}[]{@{\,}c@{\,}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\makebox[0.0pt][l]{\makebox[7.20007pt][c]{${\raisebox{0.0pt}{\mbox{{\hskip-0.2pt\rule[5.20006pt]{0.2pt}{0.4pt}\rule[5.20006pt]{7.20007pt}{0.4pt}\rule[5.20006pt]{0.2pt}{0.4pt}\hskip-7.20007pt\hskip-0.2pt}{\hskip-0.2pt\rule[-2.00002pt]{0.2pt}{0.4pt}\rule[-2.00002pt]{7.20007pt}{0.4pt}\rule[-2.00002pt]{0.2pt}{0.4pt}\hskip-7.20007pt\hskip-0.2pt}{\hskip-0.2pt\rule[-1.80002pt]{0.4pt}{7.20007pt}\hskip-0.2pt}{\hskip 7.20007pt\hskip-0.2pt\rule[-1.80002pt]{0.4pt}{7.20007pt}\hskip-0.2pt\hskip-7.20007pt}{}}}{\makebox[7.20007pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle{}$}}}$}}}\phantom{\rule[-1.80002pt]{7.20007pt}{7.20007pt}}}\end{array}}}
belongs to
\mathsf{dom}_{r+2}
. This domino is horizontal if
u_{1}>0
and vertical if
u_{1}<0
.

3.

In
Q_{\mathsf{D}}^{r}(u)
,
\mathsf{dom}_{i}
with
i\in[r+1]
is horizontal if
u_{i}>0
and vertical if
u_{i}<0
.

4.

If
u_{1}>0
, then
\mathsf{dom}_{1}
appears in
Q_{\mathsf{D}}^{r}(u)
in the positions
\{(1,r+1),(1,r+2)\}
. If
u_{1}<0
, it appears in the position
\{(r+1,1),(r+2,1)\}
.

5.

If
w_{1}>0
, assume
\{\mathsf{dom}_{i}|i=2,3,\cdots,r+2\}-{\mathrel{\begin{array}[]{@{\,}c@{\,}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\makebox[0.0pt][l]{\makebox[7.20007pt][c]{${\raisebox{0.0pt}{\mbox{{\hskip-0.2pt\rule[5.20006pt]{0.2pt}{0.4pt}\rule[5.20006pt]{7.20007pt}{0.4pt}\rule[5.20006pt]{0.2pt}{0.4pt}\hskip-7.20007pt\hskip-0.2pt}{\hskip-0.2pt\rule[-2.00002pt]{0.2pt}{0.4pt}\rule[-2.00002pt]{7.20007pt}{0.4pt}\rule[-2.00002pt]{0.2pt}{0.4pt}\hskip-7.20007pt\hskip-0.2pt}{\hskip-0.2pt\rule[-1.80002pt]{0.4pt}{7.20007pt}\hskip-0.2pt}{\hskip 7.20007pt\hskip-0.2pt\rule[-1.80002pt]{0.4pt}{7.20007pt}\hskip-0.2pt\hskip-7.20007pt}{}}}{\makebox[7.20007pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle{}$}}}$}}}\phantom{\rule[-1.80002pt]{7.20007pt}{7.20007pt}}}\end{array}}}
appears in
Q_{\mathsf{D}}^{r}(w^{\prime})
in the positions
D
, then in
Q_{\mathsf{D}}^{r}(v^{\prime})
, they appears in the positions
D^{\prime}=\{(x+1,y-1)|(x,y)\in D\}
. If
w_{1}<0
, assume
\{\mathsf{dom}_{i}|i=2,3,\cdots,r+2\}-{\mathrel{\begin{array}[]{@{\,}c@{\,}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\makebox[0.0pt][l]{\makebox[7.20007pt][c]{${\raisebox{0.0pt}{\mbox{{\hskip-0.2pt\rule[5.20006pt]{0.2pt}{0.4pt}\rule[5.20006pt]{7.20007pt}{0.4pt}\rule[5.20006pt]{0.2pt}{0.4pt}\hskip-7.20007pt\hskip-0.2pt}{\hskip-0.2pt\rule[-2.00002pt]{0.2pt}{0.4pt}\rule[-2.00002pt]{7.20007pt}{0.4pt}\rule[-2.00002pt]{0.2pt}{0.4pt}\hskip-7.20007pt\hskip-0.2pt}{\hskip-0.2pt\rule[-1.80002pt]{0.4pt}{7.20007pt}\hskip-0.2pt}{\hskip 7.20007pt\hskip-0.2pt\rule[-1.80002pt]{0.4pt}{7.20007pt}\hskip-0.2pt\hskip-7.20007pt}{}}}{\makebox[7.20007pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle{}$}}}$}}}\phantom{\rule[-1.80002pt]{7.20007pt}{7.20007pt}}}\end{array}}}
appears in
Q_{\mathsf{D}}^{r}(w^{\prime})
in the positions
D
, then in
Q_{\mathsf{D}}^{r}(v^{\prime})
, they appears in the positions
D^{\prime}=\{(x-1,y+1)|(x,y)\in D\}
.

Proof.

We only show this result for
w_{1}>0
and for
w_{2}\cdots w_{r+2}
is positive decreasing after neagative increasing, the other cases are similar.

Assume
w_{1}>0
,
w_{2}\cdots w_{j}
is negative increasing while
w_{j+1}\cdots w_{r+2}
is positive decreasing. Then after the insertion of
w_{1}
, we see that
\mathsf{dom}_{1}
appears in
Q_{\mathsf{D}}^{r}(u)
in the positions
\{(1,r+1),(1,r+2)\}
. So 4. is true.

According to the proof of Theorem 3.4 in
[
25
]
, it is easy to get the result 1. and 2.

To show the result 3. and 5., consider the insertion progress. Inserting
w_{2}\cdots w_{j}
is the same as bumping
w_{i}
into column
j+1-i
for
i=2,3,\cdots,j
. For the recording tableau, we have
\mathsf{dom}_{i}
in position
\{(i-1,r+3-i),(i-1,r+4-i)\}
.

Then, inserting
w_{j+1}\cdots w_{r+2}
is the same as bumping
w_{i}
into row
r+3-i
for
i=j+1,\cdots,r+2
and bumping
w_{1}
into row
r+3-j
.
For the recording tableau, we have
\mathsf{dom}_{i}
in position
\{(r+j+1-i,i-j+1),(r+j+2-i,i-j+1)\}
for
i=j+1,\cdots,r+1
and
\mathsf{dom}_{r+2}
in the position
(j,r+3-j),(j+1,r+3-j)
. This shows the result 3.

For the result 5., consider inserting of
\overline{w_{1}}w_{2}\cdots w_{j}
, then it is the same as bumping
w_{i}
into column
j+2-i
for
i=1,2,3,\cdots,j
. For the recording tableau, we have
\mathsf{dom}_{i}
in position
\{(i,r+2-i),(i,r+3-i)\}
for
i=1,2,3,\cdots,j
.

Then, inserting
w_{j+1}\cdots w_{r+2}
is the same as bumping
w_{i}
into row
r+2-i
for
i=j+1,\cdots,r+2
. For the recording tableau, we have
\mathsf{dom}_{i}
in position
\{(r+j+2-i,i-j),(r+j+3-i,i-j)\}
for
i=j+1,\cdots,r+1
.
These bumping processes do not affect each other except bumping
w_{1}
into column
j+1
and bumping
w_{j+1}
into row
r+1-j
, which lead to
\mathsf{dom}_{r+2}
in the position
\{(j,r+3-j),(j+1,r+3-j)\}
for the recording tableau.

Comparing the
\mathsf{dom}_{i}
in the recording tableau, we get the result 5.
∎

Proposition 3.12
.

For two signed permutations
w
,
v
in
W^{\mathsf{BC}}_{n}
, if they are related by a
\mathrm{D}_{4}^{r}
relation, say
w=uzw_{s+1}\cdots w_{m}~\sim~u^{\prime}zw_{s+1}\cdots w_{m}=v
where
s=(k+1)(k+r+1)
, then we have the following properties:

1.

If
i>s
,
\mathsf{dom}_{i}
appears in the same position of
Q_{\mathsf{D}}(w)
and
Q_{\mathsf{D}}(v)
.

2.

If
i\leq kr+k^{2}
or
kr+k^{2}+k+1\leq i<s
,
\mathsf{dom}_{i}
appears in the same position of
Q_{\mathsf{D}}(w)
and
Q_{\mathsf{D}}(v)
.

3.

If
kr+k^{2}+1\leq i\leq kr+k^{2}+k-1<s
,
\mathsf{dom}_{i}
in
Q_{\mathsf{D}}(w)
appears in the same position as
\mathsf{dom}_{i+1}
in
Q_{\mathsf{D}}(v)
.

4.

If
\mathsf{dom}_{kr+k^{2}+k}
appears in the position
\{(x,y),(x,y+1)\}
(resp.
\{(x,y),(x+1,y)\}
) in
Q_{\mathsf{D}}(w)
, then
\mathsf{dom}_{kr+k^{2}+1}
appears in the position
\{(x,y),(x+1,y)\}
(resp.
\{(x,y),(x,y+1)\}
) in
Q_{\mathsf{D}}(v)
. In this case,
\mathsf{dom}_{s}
appears in the position
\{(x+1,y),(x+1,y+1)\}
(resp.
\{(x,y+1),(x+1,y+1)\}
) in
Q_{\mathsf{D}}(w)
while
\mathsf{dom}_{s}
appears in the position
\{(x,y+1),(x+1,y+1)\}
(resp.
\{(x+1,y),(x+1,y+1)\}
) in
Q_{\mathsf{D}}(v)
. In other word, we have

Proof.

Without loss of genrality, we only consider the case when
n=m=s
,
w=uz
and
a_{i,j}>0
,
b_{i,j}<0
.

Define
u=u_{1}u_{2}\cdots u_{k+1}
, where
u_{i}=a_{{}_{i,r+i}}\cdots a_{{}_{i,1}}b_{{}_{i,i}}\cdots b_{{}_{i,1}}
for
i=1,2,\cdot,k
and
u_{k+1}=a_{{}_{k+1,r+k}}\cdots a_{{}_{k+1,1}}
. Then we see that
Q_{\mathsf{D}}(u_{1})
have
\mathsf{dom}_{i}
in position
\{(r+2-i,i),(r+3-i,i)\}
for
i=1,\cdots,r+1
and
\mathsf{dom}_{r+2}
in position
\{(1,r+2),(1,r+3)\}
. For example, for
r=2
, we have

Q_{\mathsf{D}}(u_{1})=\mathrel{\begin{array}[]{@{\,}c@{\,}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\makebox[0.0pt][l]{{\raisebox{6.44444pt}{\makebox[0.0pt][l]{{\raisebox{6.44444pt}{\makebox[0.0pt][l]{{\raisebox{6.44444pt}{\makebox[0.0pt][l]{{\raisebox{0.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\makebox[0.0pt][l]{\makebox[14.39996pt][c]{${\raisebox{0.0pt}{\mbox{{\hskip-0.2pt\rule[10.59993pt]{0.2pt}{0.4pt}\rule[10.59993pt]{14.39996pt}{0.4pt}\rule[10.59993pt]{0.2pt}{0.4pt}\hskip-14.39996pt\hskip-0.2pt}{\hskip-0.2pt\rule[-3.80003pt]{0.2pt}{0.4pt}\rule[-3.80003pt]{14.39996pt}{0.4pt}\rule[-3.80003pt]{0.2pt}{0.4pt}\hskip-14.39996pt\hskip-0.2pt}{\hskip-0.2pt\rule[-3.60004pt]{0.4pt}{14.39996pt}\hskip-0.2pt}{\hskip 0.0pt}}}\makebox[0.0pt][l]{\raisebox{0.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\makebox[0.0pt][l]{\makebox[14.39996pt][c]{${\raisebox{0.0pt}{\mbox{{}{}{\hskip-0.2pt\rule[-3.60004pt]{0.4pt}{14.39996pt}\hskip-0.2pt\hskip 14.39996pt\hskip-0.2pt\rule[-3.60004pt]{0.4pt}{14.39996pt}\hskip-0.2pt\hskip-14.39996pt\hskip-0.2pt\rule[-3.80003pt]{0.2pt}{0.4pt}\rule[-3.80003pt]{14.39996pt}{0.4pt}\rule[-3.80003pt]{0.2pt}{0.4pt}\hskip-14.39996pt\hskip-0.2pt}{}}}{\makebox[14.39996pt][c]{${}$}}}$}}}\phantom{\rule[-3.60004pt]{14.39996pt}{14.39996pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt}\end{array}}

Recursively,
Q_{\mathsf{D}}(u)
have
\mathsf{dom}_{i}
in position
\{(pr+p^{2}+p+1-i,i),(pr+p^{2}+p+2-i,i)\}
if
(p-1)r+p^{2}-p+1\leq i\leq pr+p^{2}
; in position
\{(i-pr-p^{2},(p+1)r+p^{2}+2p+1-i),(i-pr-p^{2},(p+1)r+p^{2}+2p+2-i)\}
if
pr+p^{2}+1\leq i\leq pr+p^{2}+p
.

Similarly,
Q_{\mathsf{D}}(u^{\prime})
have
\mathsf{dom}_{i}
in position
\{(pr+p^{2}+p+1-i,i),(pr+p^{2}+p+2-i,i)\}
if
(p-1)r+p^{2}-p+1\leq i\leq pr+p^{2}
for
p\leq k
; in position
\{(i-pr-p^{2},(p+1)r+p^{2}+2p+1-i),(i-pr-p^{2},(p+1)r+p^{2}+2p+2-i)\}
if
pr+p^{2}+1\leq i\leq pr+p^{2}+p
for
p<k
. For
p=k
,
Q_{\mathsf{D}}(u^{\prime})
have
\mathsf{dom}_{kr+k^{2}+1}
in position
\{(k+1,r+k+1),(k+2,r+k+1)\}
, and
\mathsf{dom}_{i}
in position
\{(i-kr-k^{2},(k+1)r+k^{2}+2k+1-i),(i-kr-k^{2},(k+1)r+k^{2}+2k+2-i)\}
if
kr+k^{2}+2\leq i\leq kr+k^{2}+k
; in position
\{((k+1)r+k^{2}+3k+2-i,i),((k+1)r+k^{2}+3k+2-i,i)\}
if
kr+k^{2}+k+1\leq i\leq s
.

For example, for
r=2
,
k=2
, we have

Q_{\mathsf{D}}(u)=\mathrel{\begin{array}[]{@{\,}c@{\,}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\makebox[0.0pt][l]{{\raisebox{6.44444pt}{\makebox[0.0pt][l]{{\raisebox{6.44444pt}{\makebox[0.0pt][l]{{\raisebox{6.44444pt}{\makebox[0.0pt][l]{{\raisebox{6.44444pt}{\makebox[0.0pt][l]{{\raisebox{6.44444pt}{\makebox[0.0pt][l]{{\raisebox{0.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\makebox[0.0pt][l]{\makebox[14.39996pt][c]{${\raisebox{0.0pt}{\mbox{{\hskip-0.2pt\rule[10.59993pt]{0.2pt}{0.4pt}\rule[10.59993pt]{14.39996pt}{0.4pt}\rule[10.59993pt]{0.2pt}{0.4pt}\hskip-14.39996pt\hskip-0.2pt}{\hskip-0.2pt\rule[-3.80003pt]{0.2pt}{0.4pt}\rule[-3.80003pt]{14.39996pt}{0.4pt}\rule[-3.80003pt]{0.2pt}{0.4pt}\hskip-14.39996pt\hskip-0.2pt}{\hskip-0.2pt\rule[-3.60004pt]{0.4pt}{14.39996pt}\hskip-0.2pt}{\hskip If
b_{k,k}=\overline{n}
, then
z
is between
a_{k+1,1}
and
b_{k,1}
. If
z>0
, then inserting
z
yields
\mathsf{dom}_{s}
in the position
\{(k+2,r+k+1),(k+2,r+k+2)\}
for
Q_{\mathsf{D}}(uz)
and position
\{(k+1,r+k+2),(k+2,r+k+2)\}
for
Q_{\mathsf{D}}(u^{\prime}z)
. If
z<0
, the result is the same. For example, for
r=2
,
k=2
, we have

Q_{\mathsf{D}}(uz)=\mathrel{\begin{array}[]{@{\,}c@{\,}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\makebox[0.0pt][l]{{\raisebox{6.44444pt}{\makebox[0.0pt][l]{{\raisebox{6.44444pt}{\makebox[0.0pt][l]{{\raisebox{6.44444pt}{\makebox[0.0pt][l]{{\raisebox{6.44444pt}{\makebox[0.0pt][l]{{\raisebox{6.44444pt}{\makebox[0.0pt][l]{{\raisebox{0.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\makebox[0.0pt][l]{\makebox[14.39996pt][c]{${\raisebox{0.0pt}{\mbox{{\hskip-0.2pt\rule[10.59993pt]{0.2pt}{0.4pt}\rule[10.59993pt]{14.39996pt}{0.4pt}\rule[10.59993pt]{0.2pt}{0.4pt}\hskip-14.39996pt\hskip-0.2pt}{\hskip-0.2pt\rule[-3.80003pt]{0.2pt}{0.4pt}\rule[-3.80003pt]{14.39996pt}{0.4pt}\rule[-3.80003pt]{0.2pt}{0.4pt}\hskip-14.39996pt\hskip-0.2pt}{\hskip-0.2pt\rule[-3.60004pt]{0.4pt}{14.39996pt}\hskip-0.2pt}{\hskip If
a_{k+1,r+k}=n
and
z
is between
a_{k,1}
and
b_{k,1}
. The result is the same as the above case according to the insertion progress.

If
a_{k+1,r+k}=n
and
z
is between
a_{k,1}
and
a_{k+1,1}
with
a_{k+1,i}<a_{k,i+1}
. The result is the same as the above case according to the insertion progress.

Therefore, we show the required result.
∎

Proposition 3.13
.

For two signed permutations
w
,
v
in
W^{\mathsf{BC}}_{n}
, if they are related by a
\mathrm{D}_{5}^{r}
relation, say
w=uzw_{s+1}\cdots w_{m}~\sim~u^{\prime}zw_{s+1}\cdots w_{m}=v
where
s=(k+1)(k+r+2)
, then we have the following properties:

1.

If
i>s
,
\mathsf{dom}_{i}
appears in the same position of
Q_{\mathsf{D}}(w)
and
Q_{\mathsf{D}}(v)
.

2.

If
i\leq kr+k^{2}+k
,
\mathsf{dom}_{i}
appears in the same position of
Q_{\mathsf{D}}(w)
and
Q_{\mathsf{D}}(v)
.

3.

If
kr+k^{2}+k+1\leq i\leq(k+1)r+(k+1)^{2}
, then
\mathsf{dom}_{i}
in
Q_{\mathsf{D}}(w)
appears in the same position as
\mathsf{dom}_{i+1}
in
Q_{\mathsf{D}}(v)
.

4.

If
\mathsf{dom}_{s-k-1}
appears in the position
\{(x,y),(x,y+1)\}
(resp.
\{(x,y),(x+1,y)\}
) in
Q_{\mathsf{D}}(w)
, then
\mathsf{dom}_{s-r-2k-1}
appears in the position
\{(x,y),(x+1,y)\}
(resp.
\{(x,y),(x,y+1)\}
) in
Q_{\mathsf{D}}(v)
. In this case,
\mathsf{dom}_{s}
appears in the position
\{(x+1,y),(x+1,y+1)\}
(resp.
\{(x,y+1),(x+1,y+1)\}
) in
Q_{\mathsf{D}}(w)
while
\mathsf{dom}_{s}
appears in the position
\{(x,y+1),(x+1,y+1)\}
(resp.
\{(x+1,y),(x+1,y+1)\}
) in
Q_{\mathsf{D}}(v)
. In other word, we have

Proof.

The proof is similar as the proof of Proposition
3.12
.
∎

4
Bitableaux correspondence

4.1
Bitableau insertion

Let us first introduce some more notation. If
\lambda
is a partition, and if
T
is a standard tableau of shape
\lambda
, we set
|T|=|\lambda|
(the number
|T|
is called the size of
T
). A
bipartition
(of
n
) is a pair
(\lambda,\mu)
of partitions (such that
|\lambda|+|\mu|=n
). A
bitableau
is a pair of tableaux. If
(T_{1},T_{2})
is a bitableau such that
T_{1}
is of
shape
\lambda
and
T_{2}
is of shape
\mu
, we say that
(\lambda,\mu)
is the shape of
(T_{1},T_{2})
and
that
|\lambda|+|\mu|
is the size of
(T_{1},T_{2})
. The bitableau is said to be
n
-standard if
T_{1}
and
T_{2}
are standard tableaux, if
|T_{1}|+|T_{2}|=n
and if the filling of
T_{1}
and
T_{2}
is the set
[n]
.

Fix a standard tableau
T
with
n
boxes.
Given a permutation
w\in S_{n}
, let
w(T)
be the
tableau formed by applying
w
to each label of
T
.
\mathfrak{row}(T)
is the row reading words of
T
, i.e., the word reading
T
row by row from bottom to top, form left to right.
For each integer
1<i<n
, the
elementary dual equivalence operator

D_{i}
is the map acting on
T
by

D_{i}(T):=\begin{cases}s_{i-1}(T)&\text{ if $i+1$ lies between $i$ and $i-1$ in }\mathfrak{row}(T),\\
s_{i}(T)&\text{ if $i-1$ lies between $i$ and $i+1$ in }\mathfrak{row}(T),\\
T&\text{ if $i$ lies between $i-1$ and $i+1$ in }\mathfrak{row}(T).\end{cases}

(4.1)

This operator is an involution and always produces another standard tableau. For example,

\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\vtop{\halign{&\opttoksa@YT={\font@YT}\getcolor@YT{\save@YT{\opttoksb@YT}}\nil@YT\getcolor@YT{\startbox@@YT\the\opttoksa@YT\the\opttoksb@YT}#\endbox@YT\cr\lower 0.39993pt\vbox{\kern 0.19997pt\hbox{\kern 0.39993pt\vbox to11.78085pt{\vss\hbox to11.38092pt{\hss$1$\hss}\vss}\kern-11.78085pt\vrule height=11.78085pt,width=0.39993pt\kern 11.38092pt\vrule For a bitableau
T=(T_{1},T_{2})
, we define the elementary dual equivalence operator as follows:

D_{i}(T):=\begin{cases}(D_{i}(T_{1}),T_{2})&\text{ if }i,i+1\in T_{1},\\
(T_{1},D_{i}(T_{2}))&\text{ if }i,i+1\in T_{2},\\
(s_{i}(T_{1}),s_{i}(T_{2}))&\text{Otherwise}.\end{cases}

(4.2)

In order to generalize the Robinson-Schensted correspondence to
W^{\mathsf{BC}}_{n}
, we work with the realisation of
W^{\mathsf{BC}}_{n}
as a subgroup of
S([-n,n])
and use the usual expression
w=w_{1}w_{2}\cdots w_{n}
to represent the elements of
W^{\mathsf{BC}}_{n}
. Thus, let
w\in W^{\mathsf{BC}}_{n}
. Then we define a pair of
n
-standard bitableaux:
(P_{\mathsf{B}}(w),Q_{\mathsf{B}}(w))
where
P_{\mathsf{B}}(w)=(P_{\mathsf{B}}^{+}(w),P_{\mathsf{B}}^{-}(w))
and
Q_{\mathsf{B}}(w)=(Q_{\mathsf{B}}^{+}(w),Q_{\mathsf{B}}^{-}(w))
and
P_{\mathsf{B}}(w),Q_{\mathsf{B}}(w)
have the same shape. This is done as follows.

Apply the RSK insertion procedure as follows: insert succesively the
numbers
|w_{i}|
into two initially empty tableaux
P_{\mathsf{B}}^{+}(w),P_{\mathsf{B}}^{-}(w)
, more precisely insert
|w_{i}|
into
P_{\mathsf{B}}^{\pm}(w)
where
\frac{w}{|w|}=\pm 1
. Note that at each step this yields a new box, located
on the
a_{i}
-th row and
b_{i}
-th column, say, of
P_{\mathsf{B}}^{\pm}(w)
. Now add a box containing
i
to
Q_{\mathsf{B}}^{\pm}(w)
on its
(a_{i},b_{i})
position.

In other word, for
w\in W^{\mathsf{BC}}_{n}
, we associate to it a biword
w=\begin{pmatrix}1&2&\cdots&n\\
w_{1}&w_{2}&\cdots&w_{n}\end{pmatrix}
. Then we can get two bi-subwords:
w^{+}
contains all biletters in
w
such that the second element is greater than 0, while
w^{-}
contains all biletters in
w
such that the second element is less than 0 and then take the absolute value of them.

Example 4.1
.

If
w=\begin{pmatrix}1&2&3&4\\
-3&4&-1&2\end{pmatrix}
, then
w^{+}=\begin{pmatrix}2&4\\
4&2\end{pmatrix}
and
w^{-}=\begin{pmatrix}1&3\\
3&1\end{pmatrix}
.

Using this notation, we can see that
P_{\mathsf{B}}^{\pm}(w)=P_{\mathsf{RSK}}(w^{\pm})
and
Q_{\mathsf{B}}^{\pm}(w)=Q_{\mathsf{RSK}}(w^{\pm})
.

Example 4.2
.

w=\begin{pmatrix}1&2&3&4&5&6&7\\
-4&3&6&-1&7&-2&5\end{pmatrix}
, then

4.2
New version of bitableau insertion

We review the concept of Knuth moves. Suppose
a<b<c
are integers.
acb
,
bac
,
bca
, and
cab
are the only four permutations not strictly increasing or strictly decreasing. A
Knuth move
on these words exchanges the
a
and
c
letters. Thus
acb
and
cab
are connected by a Knuth move, as are
bca
and
bac
.

Suppose
v,w\in S_{n}
and
i
is an integer with
1<i<n
.
We write
v\mathbin{\overset{i}{\underset{\mathsf{K}}{\scalebox{1.5}[1]{$\sim$}}}}w

and say that a
Knuth move
exists between
v
and
w

if
w
is obtained from
v
by performing a Knuth move on
v_{i-1}v_{i}v_{i+1}
, or if
v=w
when the subword
v_{i-1}v_{i}v_{i+1}
is in monotonic order.
v,w
are called
Knuth equivalent
if they are connected by a sequence of Knuth moves.

Similarly, we write
v\mathbin{\overset{i}{\underset{\mathsf{dK}}{\scalebox{1.5}[1]{$\sim$}}}}w
and say that a
dual Knuth move
exists between
v
and
w
if
v^{-1}\mathbin{\overset{i}{\underset{\mathsf{K}}{\scalebox{1.5}[1]{$\sim$}}}}w^{-1}
.
Two permutations that are connected by a sequence of (dual) Knuth moves are called
(dual) Knuth equivalent
.
For example, we have

564312\mathbin{\overset{2}{\underset{\mathsf{K}}{\scalebox{1.5}[1]{$\sim$}}}}546312\mathbin{\overset{3}{\underset{\mathsf{K}}{\scalebox{1.5}[1]{$\sim$}}}}543612

and

432615\mathbin{\overset{4}{\underset{\mathsf{dK}}{\scalebox{1.5}[1]{$\sim$}}}}532614\mathbin{\overset{3}{\underset{\mathsf{dK}}{\scalebox{1.5}[1]{$\sim$}}}}542613
.

Finally, for
y,z\in I_{n}\subset S_{n}
, we define
y\mathbin{\overset{i}{\underset{\mathsf{rB}}{\scalebox{1.5}[1]{$\sim$}}}}z
if
y\mathbin{\overset{i}{\underset{\mathsf{K}}{\scalebox{1.5}[1]{$\sim$}}}}w\mathbin{\overset{i}{\underset{\mathsf{dK}}{\scalebox{1.5}[1]{$\sim$}}}}z
for some
w\in S_{n}
. We have the following results:

Lemma 4.3

(
[
19
, Lem. 2.8]
)
.

For
y,z\in I_{n}\subset S_{n}
,
y\mathbin{\overset{i}{\underset{\mathsf{rB}}{\scalebox{1.5}[1]{$\sim$}}}}z
if and only if
P_{\mathsf{RSK}}(z)=D_{i}(P_{\mathsf{RSK}}(y))
.

Theorem 4.4

(
[
19
, Thm. 2.9]
)
.

If
y,z\in I_{n}
have
y\mathbin{\overset{i}{\underset{\mathsf{rB}}{\scalebox{1.5}[1]{$\sim$}}}}z
and

A:=\{i-1,i,i+1\}
then

z=\begin{cases}y&\text{if $y(A)\neq A$ and $y(i)$ is between $y(i-1)$ and $y(i+1)$}\\
(i-1,i)y(i-1,i)&\text{if $y(A)\neq A$ and $y(i+1)$ is between $y(i-1)$ and $y(i)$}\\
(i,i+1)y(i,i+1)&\text{if $y(A)\neq A$ and $y(i-1)$ is between $y(i)$ and $y(i+1)$ }\\
(i-1,i+1)y(i-1,i+1)&\text{if $y(A)=A$}.\end{cases}

Now we define the
column Beissinger insertion
following
[
19
]
.

Definition 4.5

(
[
19
, Def. 2.10]
)
.

Let
(i,j)
be the box of
T\xleftarrow{\hskip 1.42262pt\mathsf{RSK}\hskip 1.42262pt}a
that is not in
T
.
If
a<b

then form
T\xleftarrow{\hskip 1.42262pt\mathsf{cB}\hskip 1.42262pt}(a,b)
by adding
b
to the end of
column

j+1
of
T\xleftarrow{\hskip 1.42262pt\mathsf{RSK}\hskip 1.42262pt}a
.
If
a=b
then form
T\xleftarrow{\hskip 1.42262pt\mathsf{cB}\hskip 1.42262pt}(a,b)
by adding
b
to the end of the first
column
of
T
.

Definition 4.6

(
[
19
, Def. 2.11]
)
.

Given
z\in I_{n}
, let
(a_{1},b_{1})
, …,
(a_{q},b_{q})

be the list of pairs
(a,b)\in[n]\times[n]
with
a\leq b=z(a)
, ordered with
b_{1}<\dots<b_{q}
,
and define

P_{\mathsf{cB}}(z):=\emptyset\xleftarrow{\hskip 1.42262pt\mathsf{cB}\hskip 1.42262pt}(a_{1},b_{1})\xleftarrow{\hskip 1.42262pt\mathsf{cB}\hskip 1.42262pt}(a_{2},b_{2})\xleftarrow{\hskip 1.42262pt\mathsf{cB}\hskip 1.42262pt}\cdots\xleftarrow{\hskip 1.42262pt\mathsf{cB}\hskip 1.42262pt}(a_{q},b_{q}).

Similarly, for each
1<i<n
let
\mathbin{\overset{i}{\underset{\mathsf{cB}}{\scalebox{1.5}[1]{$\sim$}}}}
be the relation on
I_{n}

with
y\mathbin{\overset{i}{\underset{\mathsf{cB}}{\scalebox{1.5}[1]{$\sim$}}}}z
if and only if
P_{\mathsf{cB}}(y)=D_{i}(P_{\mathsf{cB}}(z))
.
For any
y\in I_{n}
and
1<i<n
there is a unique
z\in I_{n}
with
y\mathbin{\overset{i}{\underset{\mathsf{cB}}{\scalebox{1.5}[1]{$\sim$}}}}z
.

Theorem 4.7

(
[
19
, Thm. 2.13]
)
.

Suppose
y,z\in I_{n}
have
y\mathbin{\overset{i}{\underset{\mathsf{cB}}{\scalebox{1.5}[1]{$\sim$}}}}z
for some
1<i<n
. For
j\in\{i-1,i,i+1\}

let

{\mathfrak{e}}(j):=\begin{cases}y(j)&\text{if }y(j)\notin\{i-1,i,i+1\}\\
-j&\text{if }y(j)=j\\
j&\text{if }j\neq y(j)\in\{i-1,i,i+1\}.\end{cases}

(4.3)

Then it holds that

z=\begin{cases}y&\text{if ${\mathfrak{e}}(i)$ is between ${\mathfrak{e}}(i-1)$ and ${\mathfrak{e}}(i+1)$}\\
(i-1,i)y(i-1,i)&\text{if ${\mathfrak{e}}(i+1)$ is between ${\mathfrak{e}}(i-1)$ and ${\mathfrak{e}}(i)$}\\
(i,i+1)y(i,i+1)&\text{if ${\mathfrak{e}}(i-1)$ is between ${\mathfrak{e}}(i)$ and ${\mathfrak{e}}(i+1)$.}\end{cases}

Using the definitions above, we define
P_{\mathsf{B^{\prime}}}^{+}(z)=P_{\mathsf{RSK}}(z^{+})
,
P_{\mathsf{B^{\prime}}}^{-}(z)=P_{\mathsf{cB}}(z^{-})
and
Q_{\mathsf{B^{\prime}}}^{\pm}(z)=P_{\mathsf{B^{\prime}}}^{\pm}(z)
. Then we have the following results:

4.3
Admissible relations

To generalize the Knuth relation in
S_{n}
, we begin with some general definitions.

Definition 4.8
.

For two signed permutations
w
and
v
in
W^{\mathsf{BC}}_{n}
, we say
w
and
v
are
admissibly-equivalent
,
w\mathbin{\overset{}{\underset{\mathsf{A}}{\scalebox{1.5}[1]{$\sim$}}}}v
, if one of them can be obtained
from the other by applying a sequence of
\mathrm{A}_{i}
relations
for
i=1,\cdots 3
, explained below.

Let
w=w_{1}\cdots w_{n}\in W^{\mathsf{BC}}_{n}
.

\mathrm{A}_{1}^{r}
:

If
w_{i-1},w_{i},w_{i+1}
are of same signs, and
w_{i}<w_{i-1}<w_{i+1}
or
w_{i+1}<w_{i-1}<w_{i}
for some
2\leq i\leq n-1
.

\mathrm{A}_{2}^{r}
:

If
w_{i},w_{i+1},w_{i+2}
are of same signs, and
w_{i}<w_{i+2}<w_{i+1}
or
w_{i+1}<w_{i+2}<w_{i}
for some
1\leq i\leq n-2
.

\mathrm{A}_{3}^{r}
:

If there exists
1\leq i\leq n-1
such that
w_{i}
and
w_{i+1}
have opposite signs.

In all cases, we have
v=s_{i}w
.

Proposition 4.9
.

[
[
6
, Prop. 3.8]
]
For
w,v\in W^{\mathsf{BC}}_{n}
,
w\mathbin{\overset{}{\underset{\mathsf{A}}{\scalebox{1.5}[1]{$\sim$}}}}v
if and only if
P_{\mathsf{B}}(w)=P_{\mathsf{B}}(v)
.

Definition 4.10
.

For two signed involutions
y
and
z
in
I_{n}
, we say
y
and
z
are
admissibly’-equivalent
,
y\mathbin{\overset{i}{\underset{\mathsf{A^{\prime}}}{\scalebox{1.5}[1]{$\sim$}}}}z
, if one of the following cases occurs:

1.

If
i,i+1\in y^{+}
,
y^{-}=z^{-}
and
y^{+}\mathbin{\overset{i}{\underset{\mathsf{rB}}{\scalebox{1.5}[1]{$\sim$}}}}z^{+}
.

2.

If
i,i+1\in y^{-}
,
y^{+}=z^{+}
and
y^{-}\mathbin{\overset{i}{\underset{\mathsf{cB}}{\scalebox{1.5}[1]{$\sim$}}}}z^{-}
.

3.

Otherwise,
y^{+}=s_{i}(z^{+})
and
y^{-}=s_{i}(z^{-})
.

Proposition 4.11
.

For
y,z\in I_{n}
,
y\mathbin{\overset{i}{\underset{\mathsf{A^{\prime}}}{\scalebox{1.5}[1]{$\sim$}}}}z
if and only if
P_{\mathsf{B^{\prime}}}(z)=D_{i}(P_{\mathsf{B^{\prime}}}(y))
.

Proof.

In case 1, we have
y^{-}=z^{-}
and then
P_{\mathsf{B^{\prime}}}^{-}(y)=P_{\mathsf{B^{\prime}}}^{-}(z)
. According to
[
19
, Theorem 2.9, 3.15]
,
P_{\mathsf{B^{\prime}}}^{+}(y)=P_{\mathsf{B}}(y^{+})=D_{i}(P_{\mathsf{B}}(z^{+}))=D_{i}(P_{\mathsf{B^{\prime}}}^{+}(z))
and the results follows.

In case 2, we have
y^{+}=z^{+}
and then
P_{\mathsf{B^{\prime}}}^{+}(y)=P_{\mathsf{B^{\prime}}}^{+}(z)
. According to
[
19
, Theorem 2.13, 3.18]
,
P_{\mathsf{B^{\prime}}}^{-}(y)=P_{\mathsf{cB}}(y^{-})=D_{i}(P_{\mathsf{cB}}(z^{-}))=D_{i}(P_{\mathsf{B^{\prime}}}^{-}(z))
and the results follows.

In case 3,
z^{+}
and
z^{-}
are obtained from
y^{+}
and
y^{-}
by substituting
i
by
i+1
or vice versa. Then the result is followed by the insertion algorithm.

Conversely, we also have three cases. If
i,i+1
both appear in
P_{\mathsf{B^{\prime}}}^{+}(y)
, then according to
[
19
, Theorem 2.9,3.15]
, we get case 1.

If
i,i+1
both appear in
P_{\mathsf{B^{\prime}}}^{-}(y)
, then according to
[
19
, Theorem 2.13, 3.18]
, we get case 2.

If one of
i,i+1
appears in
P_{\mathsf{B^{\prime}}}^{+}(y)
and another appears in
P_{\mathsf{B^{\prime}}}^{-}(y)
, the we must have the following fact:
z^{+}
and
z^{-}
are obtained from
y^{+}
and
y^{-}
by substituting
i
by
i+1
or vice versa. Thus, we get case 3.
∎

5
Molecules in Gelfand
W
-graphs

5.1
Iwahori-Hecke algebras and
W
-graphs

Similar as the symmetric group case studied in
[
19
]
, we briefly review some general background material from
[
12
, Chapter 7]
.
The
Iwahori-Hecke algebra

\mathcal{H}=\mathcal{H}(W)
of a Coxeter system
(W,S)
with length function
\ell:W\to\mathbb{N}
is the
\mathbb{Z}[x,x^{-1}]
-algebra with basis
\{H_{w}:w\in W\}
satisfying

H_{s}H_{w}=\begin{cases}H_{sw}&\text{if }\ell(sw)>\ell(w)\\
H_{sw}+(x-x^{-1})H_{w}&\text{if }\ell(sw)<\ell(w)\end{cases}\quad\text{for }s\in S\text{ and }w\in W.

This algebra has unit
H_{1}=1
.

This algebra has many important properties, one of them is the bar operator. There is a unique ring involution of
\mathcal{H}
, written as
h\mapsto\overline{h}
and called the
bar operator
, such that
\overline{x}=x^{-1}
and
\overline{H_{s}}=H_{s}^{-1}=H_{s}-(x-x^{-1})
for all
s\in S
.
More generally, for an
\mathcal{H}
-module
\mathcal{A}
, an
\mathcal{H}
-compatible bar operator
is a
\mathbb{Z}
-linear map
\mathcal{A}\to\mathcal{A}
,
a\mapsto\overline{a}
, such that
\overline{ha}=\overline{h}\cdot\overline{a}
for all
h\in\mathcal{H}
and
a\in\mathcal{A}
.

Following the conventions in
[
24
]
, we define a
W
-graph

to be a triple
\Gamma=(V,\omega,\tau)
consisting of the vertex set
V
with maps
\omega:V\times V\to\mathbb{Z}[x,x^{-1}]
and
\tau:V\to\{\text{ subsets of $S$ }\}

such that the free
\mathbb{Z}[x,x^{-1}]
-module with basis
\{Y_{v}:v\in V\}
has a left
\mathcal{H}
-module structure in which

H_{s}Y_{v}=\begin{cases}xY_{v}&\text{if }s\notin\tau(v)\\
-x^{-1}Y_{v}+\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}w\in V\\
s\notin\tau(w)\end{subarray}}\omega(v,w)Y_{w}&\text{if }s\in\tau(v)\end{cases}\quad\text{for all $s\in S$ and $v\in V$.}

(5.1)

We view
\Gamma
as a weighted digraph with edges
v\xrightarrow{\omega(v,w)}w
for each
v,w\in V
with
\omega(v,w)\neq 0
.

Remark 5.1
.

The values of
\omega(v,w)
when
\tau(v)\subseteq\tau(w)
play no role in the formula (
5.1
).
Thus, when considering the problem of classifying
W
-graphs,
it is natural to impose the further condition (called
reducedness
in
[
24
]
) that
\omega(v,w)=0
if
\tau(v)\subseteq\tau(w)
.
Although we adopted this convention in
[
17
]
, we omit it here. This simplifies some formulas.

Example 5.2
.

The prototypical examples of
W
-graphs are the
left and right Kazhdan-Lusztig graphs
,
which are described as follows.
The
Kazhdan-Lusztig basis
of
\mathcal{H}
is the unique basis
\{\underline{H}_{w}:w\in W\}

satisfying
\underline{H}_{w}=\overline{\underline{H}_{w}}\in H_{w}+\sum_{\ell(y)<\ell(w)}x^{-1}\mathbb{Z}[x^{-1}]H_{y}
.
Let
h_{yw}\in\mathbb{Z}[x^{-1}]
be the unique polynomials such that
\underline{H}_{w}=\sum_{y\in W}h_{yw}H_{y}

and define
\mu_{yw}
to be the coefficient of
x^{-1}
in
h_{yw}
.
Then set
\omega_{\mathsf{KL}}(y,w)=\mu_{yw}+\mu_{wy}
for
y,w\in W

and define

\mathrm{Asc}_{L}(w):=\{s\in S:\ell(sw)>\ell(w)\}\quad\text{and}\quad\mathrm{Asc}_{R}(w):=\{s\in S:\ell(ws)>\ell(w)\}.

The triples
(W,\omega_{\mathsf{KL}},\mathrm{Asc}_{L})

and
(W,\omega_{\mathsf{KL}},\mathrm{Asc}_{R})
are both
W
-graphs,
whose associated
\mathcal{H}
-modules (
5.1
) are isomorphic to the left and right regular representations of
\mathcal{H}

[
13
, Thm. 1.3]
.
The edge weights of
W
-graphs are actually nonnegative; in fact, one has
h_{yw}\in\mathbb{N}[x^{-1}]

[
8
, Cor. 1.2]
.

From this point on, we specialize to the case when

\mathcal{H}=\mathcal{H}(W^{\mathsf{BC}}_{n})
or
\mathcal{H}=\mathcal{H}(W^{\mathsf{D}}_{n})
.
We say that an
\mathcal{H}
-module
\mathcal{A}
is a
Gelfand model
if the character of this specialization is the multiplicity-free sum of all irreducible characters of
W^{\mathsf{BC}}_{n}
or
W^{\mathsf{D}}_{n}
.

5.2
Gelfand models

We review the constructions of the two Gelfand models for
\mathcal{H}=\mathcal{H}(W^{\mathsf{BC}}_{n})
or
\mathcal{H}=\mathcal{H}(W^{\mathsf{D}}_{n})
.

For
\mathcal{H}=\mathcal{H}(W^{\mathsf{BC}}_{n})
, the bases of these models are indexed by two subsets of the absolutely fixed-point-free involutions
I^{\mathsf{FPF}}_{2n}
. Here
I^{\mathsf{FPF}}_{n}\subset I_{n}
such that
z\in I^{\mathsf{FPF}}_{n}
satisfies
|z(i)|\neq i
for all
i\in[n]
. Then the set
\mathcal{G}^{\mathrm{BC},\mathbf{m}}_{n}
consists of the elements
z\in I^{\mathsf{FPF}}_{2n}
with no
visible descents
greater than
n
,
where an integer
i
is a visible descent of
z
if
z(i+1)<\min\{i,z(i)\}
or
z(i)<-i

[
16
, Prop. 2.9]
. Explictly, we define the index sets as follows:

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}^{\mathrm{BC},\mathbf{m}}_{n}

\displaystyle:=\{w\in I^{\mathsf{FPF}}_{2n}\mid z(i+1)\geq\min\{i,z(i)\}\text{ for }i>n\}

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}^{\mathrm{BC},\mathbf{n}}_{n}

\displaystyle:=\{w\in I^{\mathsf{FPF}}_{2n}\mid z(i+1)\leq\max\{i+2,z(i)\}\text{ for }i>n\}

There are two bijections
\iota_{\mathsf{asc}}(w)
and
\iota_{\mathsf{des}}(w)
from
I_{n}
to
\mathcal{G}^{\mathrm{BC},\mathbf{m}}_{n}
and
\mathcal{G}^{\mathrm{BC},\mathbf{n}}_{n}
respectively. Let
1_{\mathsf{FPF}}
be the permutation of
\mathbb{Z}
sending
i\mapsto i-(-1)^{i}
.
Choose
w\in I_{n}
and let
c_{1}<c_{2}<\cdots<c_{q}
be the numbers
c\in[-n,n]
with
w(c)=|c|
.
Both
\iota_{\mathsf{asc}}(w)
and
\iota_{\mathsf{des}}(w)
will be elements of
I^{\mathsf{FPF}}_{2n}

sending

i\mapsto w(i)\text{ for $i\in[n]\setminus\{c_{1},c_{2},\dots,c_{q}\}$}\quad\text{and}\quad i\mapsto 1_{\mathsf{FPF}}(i)\text{ for $i\in[2n]\setminus[n+q]$.}

The only difference between these two permutations is that we define

\iota_{\mathsf{asc}}(w):c_{i}\leftrightarrow n+i\quad\text{and}\quad\iota_{\mathsf{des}}(w):c_{i}\leftrightarrow n+q+1-i\qquad\text{for all }i\in[q].

Example 5.3
.

If
n=4
and
w=(1,3)
then
\iota_{\mathsf{asc}}(w)=(1,3)(2,5)(4,6)(7,8)

and
\iota_{\mathsf{des}}(w)=(1,3)(2,6)(4,5)(7,8)
.
Is it useful to draw involutions in
S_{n}
as matchings on
[n]
with edges corresponding to
2
-cycles.
Our examples are given in terms of such pictures as

For
\mathcal{H}=\mathcal{H}(W^{\mathsf{D}}_{n})
, we define
\mathcal{G}^{\mathrm{D},\mathbf{m}}_{n}=\mathcal{G}^{\mathrm{BC},\mathbf{m}}_{n}\cap W^{\mathsf{D}}_{2n}
and
\mathcal{G}^{\mathrm{D},\mathbf{n}}_{n}=\mathcal{G}^{\mathrm{BC},\mathbf{n}}_{n}\cap W^{\mathsf{D}}_{2n}
.

Now fix
z\in\mathcal{G}
where
\mathcal{G}\in\{\mathcal{G}^{\mathrm{BC},\mathbf{m}}_{n},\mathcal{G}^{\mathrm{D},\mathbf{m}}_{n}\}

and define

\mathrm{Des}^{=}(z):=\{s\in S:sz=zs\}\quad\text{and}\quad\mathrm{Asc}^{=}(z):=\{s\in S:zsz\in\{s_{i}:i>n\}\}.

(5.2)

Finally let

\displaystyle\mathrm{Des}^{<}(z)

\displaystyle:=\{s\in S:\ell(sz)<\ell(z)\}-\mathrm{Des}^{=}(z)\sqcup\mathrm{Asc}^{=}(z),

(5.3)

\displaystyle\mathrm{Asc}^{<}(z)

\displaystyle:=\{s\in S:\ell(sz)>\ell(z)\}-\mathrm{Des}^{=}(z)\sqcup\mathrm{Asc}^{=}(z),

where
\ell
is the length function of
W^{\mathsf{BC}}_{2n}
, or
W^{\mathsf{D}}_{2n}
.
We refer to elements of
\mathrm{Des}^{=}(z)
and
\mathrm{Asc}^{=}(z)
as
weak descents
and
weak ascents
of
z
,
and to elements of
\mathrm{Des}^{<}(z)
and
\mathrm{Asc}^{<}(z)
as
strict descents
and
strict ascents
.

For the descents and ascents sets, we have the following explicit descriptions.

Proposition 5.4

(
[
17
, Prop 2.5]
)
.

Suppose
v\in\mathcal{G}^{\mathrm{BC},\mathbf{m}}_{n}
or
v\in\mathcal{G}^{\mathrm{D},\mathbf{m}}_{n}
.

(a)

Suppose
s=s_{i}
for
i\in[n-1]
. Then:

•

s\in\mathrm{Des}^{=}(v)
if and only if
v(i)=i+1
or
v(i)=-i-1
.

•

s\in\mathrm{Asc}^{=}(v)
if and only if
n<v(i)<v(i+1)
or
v(i)<v(i+1)<-n
.

•

s\in\mathrm{Des}^{<}(v)
if and only if
i+1\neq v(i)>v(i+1)\neq i
.

•

s\in\mathrm{Asc}^{<}(v)
in all other cases.

(b)

Suppose
W=W^{\mathsf{BC}}_{n}
. Then
s_{0}\in\mathrm{Des}^{<}(v)
if
z(1)<0
and
s_{0}\in\mathrm{Asc}^{<}(v)
if
v(1)>0
.

(c)

Suppose
W=W^{\mathsf{D}}_{n}
. Then:

•

s_{-1}\in\mathrm{Des}^{=}(v)
if and only if
v(1)=2
or
v(1)=-2
.

•

s_{-1}\in\mathrm{Asc}^{=}(v)
if and only if
v(1)<-n<n<v(2)
or
v(2)<-n<n<v(1)
.

•

s_{-1}\in\mathrm{Des}^{<}(v)
if
s_{-1}\notin\mathrm{Des}^{=}(v)\sqcup\mathrm{Asc}^{=}(v)
and
-v(2)>v(1)
.

•

s_{-1}\in\mathrm{Asc}^{<}(v)
if
s_{-1}\notin\mathrm{Des}^{=}(v)\sqcup\mathrm{Asc}^{=}(v)
and
-v(2)<v(1)
.

The next two theorems summarize the type B and D case of a few of the main results from
[
17
]
.

Theorem 5.5

(
[
17
, Thms. 1.7 and 1.8]
)
.

Let
\mathcal{H}=\mathcal{H}(W)
where
W=W^{\mathsf{BC}}_{n}
or
W=W^{\mathsf{D}}_{n}
and define
\mathcal{M}
to be the free
\mathbb{Z}[x,x^{-1}]
-module with basis
\{M_{z}:z\in\mathcal{G}^{\mathrm{BC},\mathbf{m}}_{n}\}
or
\{M_{z}:z\in\mathcal{G}^{\mathrm{D},\mathbf{m}}_{n}\}
respectively.
There is a unique
\mathcal{H}
-module structure on
\mathcal{M}
in which

H_{s}M_{z}=\begin{cases}M_{szs}&\text{if }i\in\mathrm{Asc}^{<}(z)\\
M_{szs}+(x-x^{-1})M_{z}&\text{if }i\in\mathrm{Des}^{<}(z)\\
-x^{-1}M_{z}&\text{if }i\in\mathrm{Asc}^{=}(z)\\
xM_{z}&\text{if }i\in\mathrm{Des}^{=}(z)\end{cases}\quad\text{for $s=s_{i}\in\{s_{1},\dots,s_{n-1}\}$.
}

This
\mathcal{H}
-module has the following additional properties:

(a)

When
W\neq W^{\mathsf{D}}_{2k}
for some
k\in\mathbb{Z}
,
\mathcal{M}
is a Gelfand model for
\mathcal{H}
.

(b)

\mathcal{M}
has a unique
\mathcal{H}
-compatible bar operator with
\overline{M_{z}}=M_{z}
whenever
\mathrm{Des}^{<}(z)=\varnothing
.

(c)

\mathcal{M}
has a unique basis
\{\underline{M}_{z}:z\in\mathcal{G}^{\mathrm{BC},\mathbf{m}}_{n}\}

with

\displaystyle\underline{M}_{z}=\overline{\underline{M}_{z}}\in M_{z}+\sum_{\ell(y)<\ell(z)}x^{-1}\mathbb{Z}[x^{-1}]M_{y}
.

Replacing
\mathcal{G}^{\mathrm{BC},\mathbf{m}}_{n}
by
\mathcal{G}^{\mathrm{BC},\mathbf{n}}_{n}
and
interchanging
x
and
-x^{-1}
changes Theorem
5.5
to the following:

Theorem 5.6

(
[
17
, Thms. 1.7 and 1.8]
)
.

Let
\mathcal{H}=\mathcal{H}(W)
where
W=W^{\mathsf{BC}}_{n}
or
W=W^{\mathsf{D}}_{n}
and define
\mathcal{M}
to be the free
\mathbb{Z}[x,x^{-1}]
-module with basis
\{M_{z}:z\in\mathcal{G}^{\mathrm{BC},\mathbf{n}}_{n}\}
or
\{M_{z}:z\in\mathcal{G}^{\mathrm{D},\mathbf{n}}_{n}\}
respectively.
There is a unique
\mathcal{H}
-module structure on
\mathcal{N}
in which

H_{s}N_{z}=\begin{cases}N_{szs}&\text{if }i\in\mathrm{Asc}^{<}(z)\\
N_{szs}+(x-x^{-1})N_{z}&\text{if }i\in\mathrm{Des}^{<}(z)\\
xN_{z}&\text{if }i\in\mathrm{Asc}^{=}(z)\\
-x^{-1}N_{z}&\text{if }i\in\mathrm{Des}^{=}(z)\end{cases}\quad\text{for $s=s_{i}\in\{s_{1},\dots,s_{n-1}\}$.
}

This
\mathcal{H}
-module has the following additional properties:

(a)

When
W\neq W^{\mathsf{D}}_{2k}
for some
k\in\mathbb{Z}
,
\mathcal{N}
is a Gelfand model for
\mathcal{H}
.

(b)

\mathcal{N}
has a unique
\mathcal{H}
-compatible bar operator with
\overline{N_{z}}=N_{z}
whenever
\mathrm{Des}^{<}(z)=\varnothing
.

(c)

\mathcal{N}
has a unique basis
\{\underline{N}_{z}:z\in\mathcal{G}^{\mathrm{BC},\mathbf{n}}_{n}\}

with

\displaystyle\underline{N}_{z}=\overline{\underline{N}_{z}}\in N_{z}+\sum_{\ell(y)<\ell(z)}x^{-1}\mathbb{Z}[x^{-1}]N_{y}
.

By mimicking Example
5.2
, one can turn the modules
\mathcal{M}
and
\mathcal{N}
into
W
-graphs for the symmetric group
W=W^{\mathsf{BC}}_{n}
or
W=W^{\mathsf{D}}_{n}
.

Let
\mathcal{G}^{\mathbf{m}}_{n}\in\{\mathcal{G}^{\mathrm{BC},\mathbf{m}}_{n},\mathcal{G}^{\mathrm{D},\mathbf{m}}_{n}\}
and
\mathcal{G}^{\mathbf{n}}_{n}\in\{\mathcal{G}^{\mathrm{BC},\mathbf{n}}_{n},\mathcal{G}^{\mathrm{D},\mathbf{n}}_{n}\}

Let
\mathbf{m}_{yz},\mathbf{n}_{yz}\in\mathbb{Z}[x^{-1}]

be the polynomials indexed by
y,z\in\mathcal{G}^{\mathbf{m}}_{n}
and
y,z\in\mathcal{G}^{\mathbf{n}}_{n}
, respectively, such that

\underline{M}_{z}=\sum_{y\in\mathcal{G}^{\mathbf{m}}_{n}}\mathbf{m}_{yz}M_{y}
and

\underline{N}_{z}=\sum_{y\in\mathcal{G}^{\mathbf{n}}_{n}}\mathbf{n}_{yz}N_{y}.

Write

\mu^{\mathbf{m}}_{yz}

and

\mu^{\mathbf{n}}_{yz}

for the coefficients of
x^{-1}
in
\mathbf{m}_{yz}
and
\mathbf{n}_{yz}
.
For
z\in\mathcal{G}^{\mathbf{m}}_{n}
define

\displaystyle\mathrm{Asc}^{\mathsf{row}}(z)

\displaystyle:=\{s_{i}:i\in\mathrm{Asc}^{<}(z)\sqcup\mathrm{Asc}^{=}(z)\}

(5.4)

\displaystyle=\{s_{i}:i\in[n-1]\text{ and }z(i)<z(i+1)\}

\displaystyle=\{s_{i}:i\in[n-1]\text{ and }\ell(z)<\ell(s_{i}zs_{i})\}.

For
z\in\mathcal{G}^{\mathbf{n}}_{n}
define

\displaystyle\mathrm{Asc}^{\mathsf{col}}(z)

\displaystyle:=\{s_{i}:i\in\mathrm{Asc}^{<}(z)\sqcup\mathrm{Des}^{=}(z)\}

(5.5)

\displaystyle=\{s_{i}:i\in[n-1]\text{ and }z(i)<z(i+1)\text{ or }z(i)=i+1\}

\displaystyle=\{s_{i}:i\in[n-1]\text{ and }\ell(z)\leq\ell(s_{i}zs_{i})\}.

Then let
\omega^{\mathsf{row}}:\mathcal{G}^{\mathbf{m}}_{n}\times\mathcal{G}^{\mathbf{m}}_{n}\to\mathbb{Z}
and
\omega^{\mathsf{col}}:\mathcal{G}^{\mathbf{n}}_{n}\times\mathcal{G}^{\mathbf{m}}_{n}\to\mathbb{Z}
be the maps with

\omega^{\mathsf{row}}(y,z):=\mu^{\mathbf{m}}_{yz}+\mu^{\mathbf{m}}_{zy}\quad\text{and}\quad\omega^{\mathsf{col}}(y,z):=\mu^{\mathbf{n}}_{yz}+\mu^{\mathbf{n}}_{zy}.

(5.6)

Unlike the Kazhdan-Lusztig case, these integer coefficients can be negative.

Theorem 5.7

(
[
17
, Thm. 1.10]
)
.

When
W\neq W^{\mathsf{D}}_{2k}
for
k\in\mathbb{Z}
, the triples
\Gamma^{\mathsf{row}}:=(\mathcal{G}^{\mathbf{m}}_{n},\omega^{\mathsf{row}},\mathrm{Asc}^{\mathsf{row}})
and
\Gamma^{\mathsf{col}}:=(\mathcal{G}^{\mathbf{m}}_{n},\omega^{\mathsf{col}},\mathrm{Asc}^{\mathsf{col}})
are
S_{n}
-graphs whose associated Iwahori-Hecke algebra modules are Gelfand models.

The definitions of
\omega^{\mathsf{row}}
and
\omega^{\mathsf{col}}
here are simpler than in
[
17
, Thm. 1.10]
, following the conventions in Remark
5.1
.
Also, the version of
\Gamma^{\mathsf{col}}
here differs from what is in
[
17
, Thm. 1.10]
in having
\mathcal{G}^{\mathrm{BC},\mathbf{n}}_{n}
as its vertex set. The two formulations are equivalent.

It is not very clear from our discussion how to actually compute the integers in (
5.6
).
We mention some inductive formulas from
[
17
]
that can be used for this purpose:

Proposition 5.8

(See
[
17
, Lems. 3.7, 3.15, and 3.27]
)
.

Let
z\in I^{\mathsf{FPF}}_{2n}
,
i\in\mathrm{Asc}^{<}(z)
, and
s=s_{i}
.

(a)

If
z\in\mathcal{G}^{\mathbf{m}}_{n}
then
\underline{M}_{szs}=(H_{s}+x^{-1})\underline{M}_{z}-\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\ell(y)<\ell(z),\hskip 1.42262pts\notin\mathrm{Asc}^{\mathsf{row}}(y)\end{subarray}}\mu^{\mathbf{m}}_{yz}\underline{M}_{y}.

(b)

If
z\in\mathcal{G}^{\mathbf{n}}_{n}
then
\underline{N}_{szs}=(H_{s}+x^{-1})\underline{N}_{z}-\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\ell(y)<\ell(z),\hskip 1.42262pts\notin\mathrm{Asc}^{\mathsf{col}}(y)\end{subarray}}\mu^{\mathbf{n}}_{yz}\underline{N}_{y}.

5.3
Bidirected edges and Gelfand molecules for type
\mathrm{B}

As explained in the introduction, it is a natural problem to classify the cells in a given
W
-graph,
where a
cell
means a strongly connected component.
The cells in the left and right Kazhdan-Lusztig
W
-graphs are called the
left and right cells
of
W
.

Two vertices in a
W
-graph
\Gamma=(V,\omega,\tau)
form a

bidirected edge

v\leftrightarrow w

if
\omega(v,w)\neq 0\neq\omega(w,v)
.
The
molecules
of
\Gamma

are the connected components for the
undirected graph on
V
that the retains only the bidirected edges.
These subsets do not inherit a
W
-graph structure but are easier to classify than the cells. However, we still cannot find a suitable way to classify the molecules, like we did in type A in
[
18
]
. In the following sections, we only interpret some relations of bidirected edges using the two kinds of tableau we introduced before. We will discuss type B in this section and type D in the next section.

Before this, we need a better understanding of the bidirected edges in
\Gamma^{\mathsf{row}}
and
\Gamma^{\mathsf{col}}
.
Fix an integer
1<i<n
and suppose
v,w\in S_{n}
are distinct.
Let
<
denote the
Bruhat order
on
S_{n}
. Recall that
one has
w<ws_{i}
if and only if
w(i)<w(i+1)
and
v<w
if and only if
v^{-1}<w^{-1}

[
7
, Chapter 2]
.
It follows for
z\in I^{\mathsf{FPF}}_{2n}
that
z<s_{i}zs_{i}
if and only if
z(i)<z(i+1)
,
which occurs if and only if
\ell(s_{i}zs_{i})=\ell(z)+2
.

For
y<z\in\mathcal{G}^{\mathrm{BC},\mathbf{m}}_{n}
, we define the following relations:

•

z=\mathcal{Z}_{i}(y)
if
tyt\leq y<sys=z<tzt
for some
\{s,t\}=\{s_{i-1},s_{i}\}
and at least one of
y(i),y(s(i+1))>0
.

•

z=\mathcal{Z}^{\prime}_{i}(y)
if
tyt\leq y<sys=z<tzt
for some
\{s,t\}=\{s_{i-1},s_{i}\}
and
y(i),y(s(i+1))<0
.

•

z=\mathcal{Z}_{0}(y)
if
y<s_{1}ys_{1}=z
and
y(1)<0<z(1)
for some
j\in[n-1]
.

•

z=\mathcal{Z}_{0^{\prime}}(y)
if
s_{1}ys_{1}\leq y<s_{0}ys_{0}=z<s_{1}zs_{1}
and
z(1)\neq-2
.

We also write
y=\mathcal{Z}(z)
if
z=\mathcal{Z}(y)
for the four relations above.

Proposition 5.9
.

Let
y,z\in\mathcal{G}^{\mathrm{BC},\mathbf{m}}_{n}
. Then
y\leftrightarrow z
is a bidirected edge in
\Gamma^{\mathsf{row}}
if and only if one of the following happens:

•

z=\mathcal{Z}_{i}(y)
for some
i\in[n]
.

•

z=\mathcal{Z}^{\prime}_{i}(y)
for some
i\in[n]
.

•

z=\mathcal{Z}_{0}(y)
.

•

z=\mathcal{Z}_{0^{\prime}}(y)
.

Thus, the molecules in
\Gamma^{\mathsf{row}}
is the equivalence classes of
y\leftrightarrow z
.

Proof.

We first characterize the bidirected edges in
\Gamma^{\mathsf{row}}
. Fix
y,z\in\mathcal{G}^{\mathrm{BC},\mathbf{m}}_{n}
.
Given the formula (
5.4
), the results in our previous paper Proposition
5.4
and
[
17
, Cor. 3.14 and Lem. 3.27]
assert that
y\leftrightarrow z
is a bidirected edge in
\Gamma^{\mathsf{row}}
if and only if one of the following holds:

\begin{cases}\text{for some }i\in[n-1],j\in[0,n-1],s_{i}ys_{i}\leq y<s_{j}ys_{j}=z<s_{i}zs_{i},\\
\text{for some }i\in[n-1],j\in[0,n-1],s_{i}zs_{i}\leq z<s_{j}zs_{j}=y<s_{i}ys_{i},\\
\text{for some }j\in[n-1],y<s_{j}ys_{j}=z\text{ and }y(1)<0<z(1),\\
\text{for some }j\in[n-1],z<s_{j}zs_{j}=y\text{ and }z(1)<0<y(1).\end{cases}

The first two cases can only appear if
|i-j|=1
so that
s_{i}
and
s_{j}
do not commute:
for example, if
s_{i}s_{j}=s_{j}s_{i}
and
s_{i}ys_{i}\leq y<s_{j}ys_{j}=z<s_{i}zs_{i}

then

\ell(z)<\ell(s_{i}zs_{i})=\ell(s_{i}s_{j}ys_{j}s_{i})=\ell(s_{j}s_{i}ys_{i}s_{j})\leq\ell(s_{i}ys_{i})+2\leq\ell(y)+2=\ell(z)

which is impossible, and similarly for the other case.

The last two cases can only appear if
j=1
, otherwise, we have
z(1)=s_{j}ys_{j}(1)=y(1)
.

Therefore, we show that
y\leftrightarrow z
is a bidirected edge in
\Gamma^{\mathsf{row}}
if and only if they satisfy one of the four relations.
∎

Proposition 5.10
.

For
y<z\in\mathcal{G}^{\mathrm{BC},\mathbf{m}}_{n}
, if
z=\mathcal{Z}_{i}(y)
for
i\in\{0\}\sqcup[n]
, then
P_{\mathsf{B}}(z)=D_{i}(P_{\mathsf{B}}(y))
.

Proof.

We discuss in cases to show
y\mathbin{\overset{i}{\underset{\mathsf{A^{\prime}}}{\scalebox{1.5}[1]{$\sim$}}}}z
.

If
y(i)
and
y(i+1)
have different signs, then it is the case 3 in Proposition
4.10
and we get
y\mathbin{\overset{i}{\underset{\mathsf{A^{\prime}}}{\scalebox{1.5}[1]{$\sim$}}}}z
.

If
z=\mathcal{Z}_{i}(y)
or
i\in\{0\}\sqcup[n]
, if
y(i),y(i+1)>0
, then by the inequalty, we must have
0<y(i)<y(i-1)<y(i+1)
or
0<y(i-1)<y(i+1)<y(i)
. Then we have
y^{-}=z^{-}
and
ty^{+}t\leq y^{+}<sy^{+}s=z^{+}<tz^{+}t
for some
\{s,t\}=\{s_{i-1},s_{i}\}
. Then according to
[
19
, Lem. 3.10]
, we have
y^{+}\mathbin{\overset{i}{\underset{\mathsf{rB}}{\scalebox{1.5}[1]{$\sim$}}}}z^{+}
and then
y\mathbin{\overset{i}{\underset{\mathsf{A^{\prime}}}{\scalebox{1.5}[1]{$\sim$}}}}z
by Proposition
4.10
.

Therefore, we have
y\mathbin{\overset{i}{\underset{\mathsf{A^{\prime}}}{\scalebox{1.5}[1]{$\sim$}}}}z
and the result follows from Proposition
4.11
.
∎

Proposition 5.11
.

For
y<z\in\mathcal{G}^{\mathrm{BC},\mathbf{m}}_{n}
, if
z=\mathcal{Z}^{\prime}_{i}(y)
for
i\in[n]
, then
P_{\mathsf{B}}(z)=D_{i}(P_{\mathsf{B}}(y))
.

Proof.

We discuss in cases to show
y\mathbin{\overset{i}{\underset{\mathsf{A^{\prime}}}{\scalebox{1.5}[1]{$\sim$}}}}z
.

Since
y(i),y(s(i+1))<0
, so
y^{+}=z^{+}
and it suffices to show that
y^{-}\mathbin{\overset{i}{\underset{\mathsf{cB}}{\scalebox{1.5}[1]{$\sim$}}}}z^{-}
.

If
tyt<y
, then by the inequality, we see that
y(i)<y(i-1)<y(i+1)<0
or
y(i-1)<y(i+1)<y(i)<0
, so we have
ty^{-}t>y>sy^{-}s=z^{-}>tz^{-}t
and
y^{-}\mathbin{\overset{i}{\underset{\mathsf{cB}}{\scalebox{1.5}[1]{$\sim$}}}}z^{-}
.

If
tyt=y
, then we have
ty^{-}t=y>sy^{-}s=z^{-}>tz^{-}t
and
y^{-}\mathbin{\overset{i}{\underset{\mathsf{cB}}{\scalebox{1.5}[1]{$\sim$}}}}z^{-}
.

Therefore, we have
y\mathbin{\overset{i}{\underset{\mathsf{A^{\prime}}}{\scalebox{1.5}[1]{$\sim$}}}}z
and the result follows from Proposition
4.11
.
∎

Proposition 5.12
.

For
y<z\in\mathcal{G}^{\mathrm{BC},\mathbf{m}}_{n}
, if
z=\mathcal{Z}_{i}(y)
or
z=\mathcal{Z}^{\prime}_{i}(y)
for
i\in[n]
and
tyt<y
, then
P_{\mathsf{D}}(z)=V_{j,j^{\prime}}(P_{\mathsf{D}}(y))
where
s=s_{j^{\prime}}
and
t=s_{j}
.

Proof.

If
z=\mathcal{Z}_{i}(y)
or
z=\mathcal{Z}^{\prime}_{i}(y)
for
i\in[n]
and
tyt<y
, where
s=s_{j^{\prime}}
and
t=s_{j}
, then similar as in the proof of
[
19
, Theorem 3.15]
,
y\mathbin{\overset{}{\underset{\mathsf{K}}{\scalebox{1.5}[1]{$\sim$}}}}s_{j^{\prime}}y
and
ys_{j^{\prime}}\mathbin{\overset{}{\underset{\mathsf{K}}{\scalebox{1.5}[1]{$\sim$}}}}z
. So the result follows from
3.5
.
∎

Proposition 5.13
.

For
y<z\in\mathcal{G}^{\mathrm{BC},\mathbf{m}}_{n}
, if
z=\mathcal{Z}_{0^{\prime}}(y)
, then
P_{\mathsf{D}}(z)=V_{1,0}(P_{\mathsf{D}}(y))
, where
V_{1,0}(T)
is obtained by substituting
T^{0}_{0}
with
T^{0}_{0^{\prime}}
.

T^{0}_{0}=\mathrel{\begin{array}[]{@{\,}c@{\,}}{\raisebox{6.44444pt}{\makebox[0.0pt][l]{{\raisebox{0.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\makebox[0.0pt][l]{\makebox[14.39996pt][c]{${\raisebox{0.0pt}{\mbox{{}{}{\hskip-0.2pt\rule[-3.60004pt]{0.4pt}{14.39996pt}\hskip-0.2pt\hskip-0.2pt\rule[10.59993pt]{0.2pt}{0.4pt}\rule[10.59993pt]{14.39996pt}{0.4pt}\rule[10.59993pt]{0.2pt}{0.4pt}\hskip-14.39996pt\hskip-0.2pt\hskip-0.2pt\rule[-3.80003pt]{0.2pt}{0.4pt}\rule[-3.80003pt]{14.39996pt}{0.4pt}\rule[-3.80003pt]{0.2pt}{0.4pt}\hskip-14.39996pt\hskip-0.2pt}{}}}{\makebox[14.39996pt][c]{${1}$}}}$}}}\phantom{\rule[-3.60004pt]{14.39996pt}{14.39996pt}}\raisebox{0.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\makebox[0.0pt][l]{\makebox[14.39996pt][c]{${\raisebox{0.0pt}{\mbox{{}{\hskip }~T^{0}_{0^{\prime}}=\mathrel{\begin{array}[]{@{\,}c@{\,}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\makebox[0.0pt][l]{{\raisebox{0.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\makebox[0.0pt][l]{\makebox[14.39996pt][c]{${\raisebox{0.0pt}{\mbox{{}{}{\hskip-0.2pt\rule[-3.60004pt]{0.4pt}{14.39996pt}\hskip-0.2pt\hskip
