14.39996pt\hskip-0.2pt\rule[-3.60004pt]{0.4pt}{14.39996pt}\hskip-0.2pt\hskip-14.39996pt\hskip-0.2pt\rule[10.59993pt]{0.2pt}{0.4pt}\rule[10.59993pt]{14.39996pt}{0.4pt}\rule[10.59993pt]{0.2pt}{0.4pt}\hskip-14.39996pt\hskip-0.2pt}{}}}{\makebox[14.39996pt][c]{${2}$}}}$}}}\phantom{\rule[-3.60004pt]{14.39996pt}{14.39996pt}}}}}\makebox[0.0pt][l]{\raisebox{0.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\makebox[0.0pt][l]{\makebox[14.39996pt][c]{${\raisebox{0.0pt}{\mbox{{}{}{\hskip-0.2pt\rule[-3.60004pt]{0.4pt}{14.39996pt}\hskip-0.2pt\hskip 14.39996pt\hskip-0.2pt\rule[-3.60004pt]{0.4pt}{14.39996pt}\hskip-0.2pt\hskip-14.39996pt\hskip-0.2pt\rule[-3.80003pt]{0.2pt}{0.4pt}\rule[-3.80003pt]{14.39996pt}{0.4pt}\rule[-3.80003pt]{0.2pt}{0.4pt}\hskip-14.39996pt\hskip-0.2pt}{}}}{\makebox[14.39996pt][c]{${}$}}}$}}}\phantom{\rule[-3.60004pt]{14.39996pt}{14.39996pt}}\raisebox{0.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\makebox[0.0pt][l]{\makebox[14.39996pt][c]{${\raisebox{0.0pt}{\mbox{{}{\hskip-0.2pt\rule[-3.60004pt]{0.4pt}{14.39996pt}\hskip-0.2pt\hskip 14.39996pt\hskip-0.2pt\rule[-3.60004pt]{0.4pt}{14.39996pt}\hskip-0.2pt\hskip-14.39996pt\hskip-0.2pt\rule[-3.80003pt]{0.2pt}{0.4pt}\rule[-3.80003pt]{14.39996pt}{0.4pt}\rule[-3.80003pt]{0.2pt}{0.4pt}\hskip-14.39996pt\hskip-0.2pt}{}}}{\makebox[14.39996pt][c]{${}$}}}$}}}\phantom{\rule[-3.60004pt]{14.39996pt}{14.39996pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt}\end{array}}

Proof.

Since
z=\mathcal{Z}_{0^{\prime}}(y)
, then
s_{1}ys_{1}\leq y<s_{0}ys_{0}=z<s_{1}zs_{1}
and
z(1)\neq-2
. Then we have
y(1)>0
and
y(1)>y(2)>-y(1)
. Consider
w=s_{0}y=zs_{0}
, we have
w(1)=-y(1)<0
and
|w(1)|>|w(2)|=|y(2)|
, so
y
and
w
are connected by a
\mathrm{D}_{3}^{0}
relation, and by Theorem
3.8
and Proposition
3.10
, we have
P_{\mathsf{D}}(w)=P_{\mathsf{D}}(y)
and
Q_{\mathsf{D}}(w)=V_{1,0}(Q_{\mathsf{D}}(y))
.

Similarly, we see that
z
and
w^{-1}
are connected by a
\mathrm{D}_{3}^{0}
relation, and then
P_{\mathsf{D}}(z)=P_{\mathsf{D}}(w^{-1})
and
Q_{\mathsf{D}}(z)=V_{1,0}(Q_{\mathsf{D}}(w^{-1}))
.

Combining these results, we have
P_{\mathsf{D}}(z)=V_{1,0}(P_{\mathsf{D}}(y))
.
∎

We want to find a single model to determine the bidirected edges, which is much better than separated into two models as above. However, both the domino tableaux and bitableaux model failed to describe all the bidirected edge relations.

5.4
Bidirected edges and Gelfand molecules for type
\mathrm{D}

For Type
\mathrm{D}
group
W^{\mathsf{D}}_{n}
, recall that
\mathcal{G}^{\mathrm{D},\mathbf{m}}_{n}
is the subset of

z\in\mathcal{G}^{\mathrm{BC},\mathbf{m}}_{n}
for which
|\{i\in[n]:z(i)<-i\}|
is even.

For
y<z\in\mathcal{G}^{\mathrm{D},\mathbf{m}}_{n}
, we define the following relations:

•

z=\mathcal{Z}_{i}(y)
if
tyt\leq y<sys=z<tzt
for some
\{s,t\}=\{s_{i-1},s_{i}\}
.

•

z=\mathcal{Z}_{2^{\prime}}(y)
if
s_{2}ys_{2}\leq y<s_{-1}ys_{-1}=z<s_{2}zs_{2}
.

•

z=\mathcal{Z}_{-1}(y)
if
s_{-1}ys_{-1}=y<s_{2}ys_{2}=z
and
y(3)>3
.

•

z=\mathcal{Z}_{-1^{\prime}}(y)
if
s_{-1}ys_{-1}<y<s_{2}ys_{2}=z
and
y(1)<-n<n<y(3)
and
0<y(2)\leq n
.

•

z=\mathcal{Z}_{-1^{\prime\prime}}(y)
if
s_{-1}ys_{-1}<y<s_{2}ys_{2}=z
and
y(3)>y(2)
and each of the following two sets of conditions are not satisfied simultaneously:
|y(1)|,|y(2)|>n
,
y(1)y(2)<0
;
|y(1)|,|y(3)|>n
,
y(1)y(3)<0
.

We also write
y=\mathcal{Z}(z)
if
z=\mathcal{Z}(y)
for the five relations above.

Proposition 5.14
.

Let
y,z\in\mathcal{G}^{\mathrm{D},\mathbf{m}}_{n}
. Then
y\leftrightarrow z
is a bidirected edge in
\Gamma^{\mathsf{row}}
if and only if one of the following happens:

•

z=\mathcal{Z}_{i}(y)
for some
i\in[n]
.

•

z=\mathcal{Z}_{2^{\prime}}(y)
.

•

z=\mathcal{Z}_{-1}(y)
.

•

z=\mathcal{Z}_{-1^{\prime}}(y)
.

•

z=\mathcal{Z}_{-1^{\prime\prime}}(y)
.

Thus, the molecules in
\Gamma^{\mathsf{row}}
is the equivalence classes of
y\leftrightarrow z
.

Proof.

This comes directly from our previous paper Proposition
5.4
and
[
17
, Cor. 3.14 and Lem. 3.27]
.
∎

Proposition 5.15
.

For
y<z\in\mathcal{G}^{\mathrm{D},\mathbf{m}}_{n}
, if
z=\mathcal{Z}_{i}(y)
for
i\in[n]
, then
P_{\mathsf{B}}(z)=D_{i}(P_{\mathsf{B}}(y))
.

Proof.

This is just a corollary of Proposition
5.10
and Proposition
5.11
.
∎

Proposition 5.16
.

For
y<z\in\mathcal{G}^{\mathrm{D},\mathbf{m}}_{n}
, if
z=\mathcal{Z}_{i}(y)
for
i\in[n]
and
tyt<y
, then
P_{\mathsf{D}}(z)=V_{j,j^{\prime}}(P_{\mathsf{D}}(y))
where
s=s_{j^{\prime}}
and
t=s_{j}
.

Proof.

This is just a corollary of Proposition
5.12
.
∎

Proposition 5.17
.

For
y<z\in\mathcal{G}^{\mathrm{D},\mathbf{m}}_{n}
, if
z=\mathcal{Z}_{-1}(y)
,
z=\mathcal{Z}_{-1^{\prime}}(y)
or
z=\mathcal{Z}_{-1^{\prime\prime}}(y)
, then
P_{\mathsf{B}}(z)=D_{2}(P_{\mathsf{B}}(y))
.

Proof.

1.

z=\mathcal{Z}_{-1}(y)
and
y(1)=2
, then we have
z^{-}=y^{-}
. Also, we can write the one-line notation of
z^{+}
and
y^{+}
:
y^{+}=21y(3)\cdots 3\cdots
and
z^{+}=3y(3)1\cdots 2\cdots
. Therefore,
z^{+}=s_{2}y^{+}s_{2}
. So the result follows from Proposition
4.11
.

2.

z=\mathcal{Z}_{-1}(y)
and
y(1)=-2
, then we have
y^{-}=21\cdots
while
z^{-}=31\cdots
. Also, we have
y^{+}=y(3)\cdots 3\cdots
and
z^{+}=y(3)\cdots 2\cdots
. Therefore,
z^{-}=s_{2}y^{-}
and
z^{+}=s_{2}y^{+}
. So the result follows from Proposition
4.11
.

3.

z=\mathcal{Z}_{-1^{\prime}}(y)
, then we have
z^{-}=y^{-}
. Also, we have
y^{+}=y(2)y(3)\cdots 2\cdots 3\cdots
and
z^{+}=y(3)y(2)\cdots 3\cdots 2\cdots
. Therefore,
z^{+}=s_{2}y^{+}s_{2}
. So the result follows from Proposition
4.11
.

4.

z=\mathcal{Z}_{-1^{\prime\prime}}(y)
and
y(2)y(3)>0
. We have
z=y(1)y(3)y(2)\cdots
and it is easy to see that
z^{-}=y^{-}
,
z^{+}=s_{2}y^{+}s_{2}
or
z^{+}=y^{+}
,
z^{-}=s_{2}y^{-}s_{2}
. So the result follows from Proposition
4.11
.

5.

z=\mathcal{Z}_{-1^{\prime\prime}}(y)
and
y(2)<0<y(3)
. We have
z=y(1)y(3)y(2)\cdots
and

y^{-}=\cdots-y(2)\cdots 2\cdots
while
z^{-}=\cdots-y(2)\cdots 3\cdots
. Also, we have
y^{+}=\cdots y(3)\cdots 3\cdots
and
z^{+}=\cdots y(3)\cdots 2\cdots
. Therefore,
z^{-}=s_{2}y^{-}
and
z^{+}=s_{2}y^{+}
. So the result follows from Proposition
4.11
.

∎
